# Vladimir Putin

"Putin" redirects here. For other uses, see [Putin (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Putin_\(disambiguation\)).

In this name that follows [East Slavic naming customs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Slavic_naming_customs), the [patronymic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patronymic) is&#x20;*&#x20;Vladimirovich* and the [family name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surname) is&#x20;*&#x20;Putin*.

| [v](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:Putin_sidebar)[t](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk\:Putin_sidebar)[e](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Template\:Putin_sidebar) |

**Vladimir Vladimirovich Putin**[^cite_note-18] (born 7 October 1952) is a Russian politician and former intelligence officer who has served as [President of Russia](239646.md) since 2012, having previously served from 2000 to 2008. Putin also served as [Prime Minister of Russia](339344.md) from 1999 to 2000[^cite_note-19] and again from 2008 to 2012.[^cite_note-20][^cite_note-21] He has been described as the *[de facto](43594.md)* leader of Russia since 1999.[^cite_note-22]

Born in Leningrad (now [Saint Petersburg](24320051.md)), Putin worked as a [KGB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KGB) foreign intelligence officer for 16 years, rising to the rank of [lieutenant colonel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lieutenant_colonel_\(Eastern_Europe\)). He resigned in 1991 to begin a political career in Saint Petersburg. In 1996, Putin moved to Moscow to join the administration of President [Boris Yeltsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Yeltsin). He briefly served as the director of the [Federal Security Service](385990.md) (FSB) and then as [secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_the_Security_Council_of_Russia) of the [Security Council of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Security_Council_of_Russia) before [being appointed prime minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Putin's_rise_to_power) in August 1999. Following Yeltsin's resignation, Putin became [acting president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acting_President_of_Russia) and, less than three months later in March 2000, [was elected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Russian_presidential_election) to his first term as president. He [was reelected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Russian_presidential_election) in 2004. Due to constitutional limitations on two consecutive presidential terms, Putin served as prime minister again from 2008 to 2012 under [Dmitry Medvedev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Medvedev). He returned to the presidency in 2012, following [an election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Russian_presidential_election) marked by allegations of fraud and [protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011%E2%80%932013_Russian_protests), and [was reelected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Russian_presidential_election) in 2018.

During Putin's initial presidential tenure, the [Russian economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_economy) grew on average by seven percent per year as a result of [several economic reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_policy_of_Vladimir_Putin#Economic,_industrial,_and_energy_policies) and [a fivefold increase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000s_commodities_boom) in the [price of oil](5137675.md) and gas.[^cite_note-Putin_2007-23][^cite_note-Fragile_Empire_2013_page_17-24] Additionally, Putin led Russia in [a conflict against Chechen separatists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Chechen_War), re-establishing federal control over the region.[^cite_note-25][^cite_note-26] While serving as prime minister under Medvedev, he oversaw the [Russo-Georgian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Georgian_War), alongside enacting [military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Russian_military_reform) and [police reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_police_reform). In his third presidential term, Russia occupied and [annexed Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annexation_of_Crimea_by_the_Russian_Federation) as well as supported [a war in eastern Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Donbas_\(2014%E2%80%932022\)) through several military incursions, resulting in international sanctions, which, together with [a drop in oil prices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010s_oil_glut) on the international markets, led to the [financial crisis in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_financial_crisis_\(2014%E2%80%932016\)).[^cite_note-27] Additionally, he ordered [a military intervention in Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_intervention_in_the_Syrian_civil_war) to support his ally, President [Bashar al-Assad](364813.md), during the [Syrian civil war](30741795.md). In April 2021, after [a referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Russian_constitutional_referendum), he [signed constitutional amendments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_amendments_to_the_Constitution_of_Russia) into law that included one allowing him to run for reelection twice more, potentially extending his presidency to 2036.[^cite_note-28][^cite_note-29] In February 2022, during his fourth presidential term, Putin launched [a full-scale invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md), which prompted [international condemnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactions_to_the_2022_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) and led to [expanded sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_sanctions_during_the_2022_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine). In September 2022, he announced [a partial mobilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Russian_mobilization) and forcibly [annexed four Ukrainian oblasts into Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_annexation_of_Donetsk,_Kherson,_Luhansk_and_Zaporizhzhia_oblasts). In March 2023, the [International Criminal Court](14880.md) issued [an arrest warrant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Court_arrest_warrants_for_Russian_figures) for Putin for [war crimes](48626.md)[^cite_note-30] related to his alleged criminal responsibility for [illegal child abductions during the war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_abductions_in_the_Russo-Ukrainian_War).[^cite_note-31] In March 2024, he [was reelected](56748201.md) to another term in an election beset by record levels of fraud.[^cite_note-NovayaGazeta_at_least_22M-32][^cite_note-novayagazeta-33][^cite_note-Meduza_breaks_down_evidence-34][^cite_note-MoscowTimesGolos2024-35]

[Under Putin's rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia_under_Vladimir_Putin), the Russian political system has been transformed into an [authoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism) [dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dictatorship) with a [personality cult](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cult_of_personality). His rule has been marked by [endemic corruption](27352290.md) and widespread [human rights violations](785595.md), including the imprisonment and suppression of [political opponents](38212364.md), intimidation and censorship of [independent media in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_freedom_in_Russia), and a lack of [free and fair elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_and_fair_election).[^cite_note-Gill-20162-36][^cite_note-Reuter-20172-37][^cite_note-38] Russia has consistently received very low scores on [Transparency International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transparency_International)'s [Corruption Perceptions Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index), *[The Economist Democracy Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist_Democracy_Index)*, [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House)'s *[Freedom in the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_in_the_World)* index, and the [Reporters Without Borders](526054.md)' [World Press Freedom Index](8264708.md).

## Early life and education

Putin was born on 7 October, 1952, in Leningrad, now known as [Saint Petersburg](24320051.md), in the [Russian SFSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_SFSR) of the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union).[^cite_note-39] He is the youngest of three children born to Vladimir Spiridonovich Putin and Maria Ivanovna Putina ([née](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_name#Maiden_and_married_names) Shelomova). Both of Putin's parents were born in 1911 in neighbouring villages in the [Tver region](463952.md); they married in 1928 and moved to Leningrad in 1932.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESakwa20082-40] His grandfather, [Spiridon Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiridon_Putin), was a personal cook to [Vladimir Lenin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Lenin) and [Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin).[^cite_note-41][^cite_note-42] Putin's birth was preceded by the deaths of two brothers: Albert, born in the 1930s, died in infancy, and Viktor, born in 1940, died of [diphtheria](58937.md) and [starvation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starvation) in 1942 during the [Siege of Leningrad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Leningrad) by [German forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wehrmacht) in [World War II](32927.md).[^cite_note-43][^cite_note-44] The family lived in a communal apartment at 12 Baskov Lane, where several families shared basic facilities; Putin's parents had moved there in 1944 into housing provided through the factory where his father worked.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESakwa20082-40]

Putin's mother was a factory worker, and his father was a [conscript](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscript) in the [Soviet Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Navy), serving in the [submarine](28825.md) fleet in the early 1930s. During the early stage of the [Nazi invasion of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Barbarossa), his father served in the [destruction battalion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Destruction_battalion) of the [NKVD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NKVD).[^cite_note-first-person2-45][^cite_note-46][^cite_note-47] Later, he was transferred to the regular army and was severely wounded in 1942.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESakwa20083-48] Putin's maternal grandmother was [killed by the German occupiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II_casualties_of_the_Soviet_Union) of the [Tver](372316.md) region in 1941, and his maternal uncles disappeared on the [Eastern Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Front_\(World_War_II\)) during World War II.[^cite_note-49]

### Education



Putin, c. 1960s

On 1 September 1960, Putin started at School No. 193 at Baskov Lane, near his home. He was one of a few in his class of about 45 pupils who were not yet members of the [Young Pioneer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Lenin_All-Union_Pioneer_Organization) (*[Komsomol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Komsomol)*) organization. At the age of 12, he began to practice [sambo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sambo_\(martial_art\)) and judo.[^cite_note-50] In his free time, he enjoyed reading the works of [Karl Marx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Marx), [Friedrich Engels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Engels), and Lenin.[^cite_note-51] Putin attended Saint Petersburg High School 281 with a German language immersion program.[^cite_note-52] He is fluent in German and has given speeches and interviews in that language.[^cite_note-53]

Putin studied law at the Leningrad State University named after [Andrei Zhdanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrei_Zhdanov) (now [Saint Petersburg State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Petersburg_State_University)) in 1970 and graduated in 1975.[^cite_note-hoffman-54] His thesis was on "The [Most Favored Nation Trading Principle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Most_favoured_nation) in International Law."[^cite_note-55] While there, he was required to join the [Communist Party of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_the_Soviet_Union) (CPSU); he remained a member until it ceased to exist in 1991.[^cite_note-56] Putin also met [Anatoly Sobchak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatoly_Sobchak), an assistant professor who taught [business law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_law),[^cite_note-57] who later became the co-author of the [Russian constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_constitution). Putin was influential in Sobchak's career in Saint Petersburg, and Sobchak was influential in Putin's career in Moscow.[^cite_note-Vlast-58]

In 1997, Putin received a degree in economics (*[Candidate of Economic Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candidate_of_Sciences)*) at the [Saint Petersburg Mining University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Petersburg_Mining_University) for a thesis on energy dependencies and their instrumentalisation in foreign policy.[^cite_note-mvart1-59][^cite_note-dwd24-60] His supervisor was [Vladimir Litvinenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Litvinenko), who in 2000 and again in 2004 managed his presidential election campaigns in St Petersburg.[^cite_note-idcg06-61] [Igor Danchenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igor_Danchenko) and Clifford Gaddy consider Putin to be a [plagiarist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plagiarist) according to Western standards. One book from which he copied entire paragraphs is the Russian-language edition of [King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Richard_King) and [Cleland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_I._Cleland)'s *Strategic Planning and Policy* (1978).[^cite_note-idcg06-61] Balzer wrote on the Putin thesis and Russian energy policy and concludes along with Olcott that "The primacy of the Russian state in the country's energy sector is non-negotiable", and cites the insistence on majority Russian ownership of any joint-venture, particularly since [BASF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BASF) signed the Gazprom [Nord Stream](71965880.md)-[Yuzhno-Russkoye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuzhno-Russkoye) deal in 2004 with a 49–51 structure, as opposed to the older 50–50 split of [BP](18998720.md)'s [TNK-BP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TNK-BP) project.[^cite_note-balzer05-62]

## Intelligence career



Putin in the [KGB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KGB) c. 1980

In 1975, Putin joined the [KGB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KGB) and trained at the 401st KGB School in Okhta, [Leningrad](480845.md).[^cite_note-stp-63] After training, he worked in the Second Chief Directorate ([counterintelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterintelligence)) before he was transferred to the [First Chief Directorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Chief_Directorate), where he monitored foreigners and consular officials in Leningrad.[^cite_note-stp-63][^cite_note-64][^cite_note-hoffman-54] In September 1984, Putin was sent to Moscow for further training at the [Yuri Andropov Red Banner Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_of_Foreign_Intelligence).[^cite_note-Hutchins2012-65][^cite_note-Jack2005-66][^cite_note-PutinGevorkyan2000-67]



The Stasi identity card of Vladimir Putin, who worked in [Dresden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dresden) as a KGB liaison officer to the Stasi

[^cite_note-BBCStasi2018-68]

From 1985 to 1990, he served in [Dresden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dresden), [East Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Germany),[^cite_note-69] using a [cover identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cover_identity) as a translator.[^cite_note-M._Gessen_p._60-70] While posted in Dresden, Putin served as one of the KGB's liaison officers to the [Stasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stasi) secret police and was reportedly promoted to [lieutenant colonel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lieutenant_colonel). According to the official Kremlin presidential site, the East German [communist regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Regime) commended Putin with a bronze medal for "faithful service to the [National People's Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_People's_Army)." Putin has publicly conveyed delight over his activities in Dresden, once recounting his confrontations with East Germany's [anti-communist protestors of 1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peaceful_Revolution) who attempted to [occupy the city's Stasi buildings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorial_and_Education_Centre_Andreasstrasse#Occupation_of_the_Stasi_headquarters_and_prison).[^cite_note-BBCStasi2018-68]

"Putin and his colleagues were reduced mainly to collecting [press clippings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_clippings), thus contributing to the mountains of useless information produced by the KGB", Russian-American [Masha Gessen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masha_Gessen) wrote in their 2012 biography of Putin.[^cite_note-M._Gessen_p._60-70] His work was also downplayed by former [Stasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stasi) spy chief [Markus Wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Markus_Wolf) and Putin's former KGB colleague Vladimir Usoltsev. Journalist [Catherine Belton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_Belton) wrote in 2020 that this downplaying was actually a cover for Putin's involvement in KGB coordination and support for the terrorist [Red Army Faction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army_Faction) (RAF), whose members frequently hid in East Germany with the support of the Stasi. Dresden was preferred as a "marginal" town with only a small presence of Western intelligence services.[^cite_note-politico1-71] According to an anonymous source who claimed to be a former RAF member, at one of these meetings in Dresden, the militants presented Putin with a list of weapons that were later delivered to the RAF in West Germany. Klaus Zuchold, who claimed to be recruited by Putin, said that Putin handled a [neo-Nazi](54361.md), Rainer Sonntag, and attempted to recruit the author of a study on poisons.[^cite_note-politico1-71] Putin reportedly met Germans to be recruited for wireless communications affairs, together with an interpreter. He was involved in wireless communications technologies in South-East Asia due to trips of German engineers, recruited by him, there and to the West.[^cite_note-hoffman-54] However, a 2023 investigation by *[Der Spiegel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Der_Spiegel)* reported that the anonymous source had never been an RAF member and is "considered a notorious fabulist" with "several previous convictions, including for making false statements."[^cite_note-72]

According to Putin's official biography, during the [fall of the Berlin Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Berlin_Wall) that began on 9 November 1989, he saved the files of the Soviet Cultural Center (House of Friendship) and of the KGB villa in Dresden for the official authorities of the would-be united Germany to prevent demonstrators, including KGB and Stasi agents, from obtaining and destroying them. He then supposedly burnt only the KGB files, in a few hours, but saved the archives of the Soviet Cultural Center for the German authorities. Nothing is written about the selection criteria during this burning; for example, regarding Stasi files or files of other agencies of the German Democratic Republic or of the USSR. He explained that many documents were left in Germany only because the furnace burst; however, many documents of the KGB villa were sent to Moscow.[^cite_note-73]

After the [collapse of the Communist East German government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Die_Wende), Putin was to resign from active KGB service because of suspicions aroused regarding his loyalty during demonstrations in Dresden and earlier, although the KGB and the [Soviet Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Ground_Forces) still operated in eastern Germany. He returned to Leningrad in early 1990 as a member of the "active reserves", where he worked for about three months with the International Affairs section of [Leningrad State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Petersburg_State_University), reporting to Vice-Rector [Yuriy Molchanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuriy_Molchanov), while working on his doctoral dissertation.[^cite_note-hoffman-54]

There, he looked for new KGB recruits, watched the student body, and renewed his friendship with his former professor, [Anatoly Sobchak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatoly_Sobchak), who was soon afterward elected the [Mayor of Leningrad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Saint_Petersburg). Putin said that he resigned with the rank of lieutenant colonel on 20 August 1991,[^cite_note-R._Sakwa_p._10-74] on the second day of the [1991 Soviet coup d'état attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Soviet_coup_d'%C3%A9tat_attempt) against Soviet president [Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev).[^cite_note-75] Putin stated: "As soon as the coup began, I immediately decided which side I was on", although he said that the choice was hard because he had spent the best part of his life with "the organs".[^cite_note-76]

## Political career

Main articles: [Political career of Vladimir Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_career_of_Vladimir_Putin) and [Vladimir Putin's rise to power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Putin's_rise_to_power)

Further information: [Russia under Vladimir Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia_under_Vladimir_Putin), [Putinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Putinism), [List of speeches given by Vladimir Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_speeches_given_by_Vladimir_Putin), and [Politics of Russia](25704.md)



Putin's official portrait as president, 2012.

Putin's political rise began in the Saint Petersburg administration (1990–1996), where in May 1990, he was appointed as an advisor on international affairs to Mayor [Anatoly Sobchak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatoly_Sobchak). Shortly thereafter, in June 1991, he became the head of the Committee for External Relations of the Saint Petersburg Mayor's Office, overseeing the promotion of international ties, foreign investment, and the registration of business ventures. Though his tenure was marred by investigations from the city legislative council concerning discrepancies in asset valuation and the export of metals, Putin retained his position until 1996. During the mid-1990s, he expanded his responsibilities in Saint Petersburg, serving as first deputy head of the city administration and leading the local branch of the pro-government political party [Our Home Is Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Our_Home_Is_Russia), as well as participating in advisory roles with regional newspapers.



Putin as director of the Federal Security Service (FSB), 1998

Transitioning to the national scene in 1996, Putin was called to Moscow following the electoral defeat of Sobchak, where he assumed the role of Deputy Chief of the Presidential Property Management Department. In this capacity, he was responsible for managing the transfer of former Soviet assets to the Russian Federation. His career in Moscow advanced rapidly with his appointment in 1997 as deputy chief of the Presidential Staff and later as chief of the Main Control Directorate of the same department. A pivotal moment came in 1998 when President [Boris Yeltsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Yeltsin) appointed him director of the [FSB](385990.md), Russia's primary intelligence and security agency. In this role, Putin concentrated on reorganising and strengthening the agency after years of perceived decline, a period that would prove formative for his later approach to governance.

In 1999, Putin described [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism) as "a blind alley, far away from the mainstream of civilization".[^cite_note-NYorker_eclipse-77] By 1999, [Zyuganov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gennady_Zyuganov) was the evident frontrunner for the first round of the pending 2000 presidential election.[^cite_note-simes-78] However, by the autumn of 1999, Vladimir Putin had overtaken Zyuganov as leading candidate in the polls.[^cite_note-rew4-79]

In August 1999, Putin's profile increased substantially when he was named one of the three First Deputy Prime Ministers, and later the acting Prime Minister following the dismissal of Sergei Stepashin's cabinet. Endorsed by Yeltsin as his preferred successor, Putin quickly capitalized on his law-and-order reputation and rose in popularity, winning the presidential election in March 2000 and being inaugurated on 7 May 2000. Throughout his subsequent terms, alternately serving as president and Prime Minister, Putin has overseen extensive reforms aimed at consolidating state power, restructuring federal relations, and curbing the influence of oligarchs. His tenure has been punctuated by significant foreign policy actions, including the controversial annexation of [Crimea](163045.md) in 2014, military interventions in [Syria](57134800.md), and ongoing involvement in the [Russo-Ukrainian war](42085878.md), including a [full-scale war with Ukraine](79400268.md) since 2022.

## Domestic policies

Main article: [Domestic policy of Vladimir Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_policy_of_Vladimir_Putin)

See also: [Freedom of assembly in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_assembly_in_Russia), [Media freedom in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_freedom_in_Russia), and [Internet censorship in Russia](37529240.md)

Further information: [2011–2013 Russian protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011%E2%80%932013_Russian_protests), [2017–2018 Russian protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017%E2%80%932018_Russian_protests), and [Bolotnaya Square case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolotnaya_Square_case)

Putin's domestic policies, particularly early in his first presidency, were aimed at creating a vertical [power structure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_structure). On 13 May 2000, he issued a decree organizing the 89 [federal subjects of Russia](701438.md) into seven administrative [federal districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_districts_of_Russia) and appointed a presidential envoy responsible for each of those districts (whose official title is Plenipotentiary Representative).[^cite_note-80]



Putin (center) with his wife Ludmila and former U.S. presidents [Bill Clinton](3356.md) and [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush) at the [state funeral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Boris_Yeltsin) of [Boris Yeltsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Yeltsin) on 25 April 2007

According to [Stephen White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_K._White), under the presidency of Putin, Russia made it clear that it had no intention of establishing a "second edition" of the American or British political system, but rather a system that was closer to Russia's own traditions and circumstances.[^cite_note-81] Some commentators have described Putin's administration as a "[sovereign democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereign_democracy)".[^cite_note-82][^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84] According to the proponents of that description (primarily [Vladislav Surkov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladislav_Surkov)), the government's actions and policies ought above all to enjoy popular support within Russia itself and not be directed or influenced from outside the country.[^cite_note-85]

The practice of the system is characterized by Swedish economist [Anders Åslund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anders_%C3%85slund) as manual management, commenting: "After Putin resumed the presidency in 2012, his rule is best described as 'manual management' as the Russians like to put it. Putin does whatever he wants, with little consideration for the consequences, with one important caveat. During the Russian financial crash of August 1998, Putin learned that financial crises are politically destabilizing and must be avoided at all costs. Therefore, he cares about financial stability".[^cite_note-86]

The period after 2012 saw mass protests against the falsification of elections, censorship, and the toughening of free assembly laws. In July 2000, according to a law proposed by Putin and approved by the [Federal Assembly of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Assembly_of_Russia), Putin gained the right to dismiss the heads of the 89 federal subjects. In 2004, the direct election of those [heads (usually called "governors")](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_current_heads_of_federal_subjects_of_Russia) by popular vote was replaced with a system whereby they would be nominated by the president and approved or disapproved by regional legislatures.[^cite_note-87][^cite_note-88]

This was seen by Putin as a necessary move to stop separatist tendencies and get rid of those governors who were connected with organized crime.[^cite_note-89] This and other government actions effected under Putin's presidency have been criticized by many independent Russian media outlets and Western commentators as anti-democratic.[^cite_note-90][^cite_note-91]

During his first term in office, Putin opposed some of the Yeltsin-era [business oligarchs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_oligarch), as well as his political opponents, resulting in the exile or imprisonment of such people as [Boris Berezovsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Berezovsky_\(businessman\)), [Vladimir Gusinsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Gusinsky), and [Mikhail Khodorkovsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Khodorkovsky); other oligarchs such as [Roman Abramovich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Abramovich) and [Arkady Rotenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arkady_Rotenberg) are friends and allies with Putin.[^cite_note-92] Putin succeeded in codifying land law and tax law and promulgated new codes on labour, administrative, criminal, commercial, and civil procedural law.[^cite_note-sharlet-93] Under Medvedev's presidency, Putin's government implemented some key reforms in the area of state security, the [Russian police reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_police_reform) and the [Russian military reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_military_reform).[^cite_note-94] During Putin's rule, the [Communist Party of the Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_the_Russian_Federation) has changed from radical, civil war-threatening to a component of the "systemic opposition" with criticism rarely more than rhetorical.[^cite_note-95]

### Economic, industrial, and energy policies

See also: [Economy of Russia](25705.md), [Energy policy of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_policy_of_Russia), [Great Recession in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession_in_Russia), [Russian financial crisis (2014–2016)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_financial_crisis_\(2014%E2%80%932016\)), and [Economic impact of the 2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_impact_of_the_2022_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine)

[Sergey Guriyev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Guriyev), when talking about Putin's economic policy, divided it into four distinct periods: the "reform" years of his first term (1999–2003); the "statist" years of his second term (2004—the first half of 2008); the world economic crisis and recovery (the second half of 2008–2013); and the Russo-Ukrainian War, Russia's growing isolation from the global economy, and stagnation (2014–present).[^cite_note-96]

In 2000, Putin launched the "Programme for the Socio-Economic Development of the Russian Federation for the Period 2000–2010", but it was abandoned in 2008 when it was 30% complete.[^cite_note-97] Fueled by the [2000s commodities boom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000s_commodities_boom) including [record-high oil prices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_oil_market_chronology_from_2003),[^cite_note-Putin_2007-23][^cite_note-Fragile_Empire_2013_page_17-24] under the Putin administration from 2000 to 2016, an increase in income in USD terms was 4.5 times.[^cite_note-98] During Putin's first eight years in office, industry grew substantially, as did production, construction, real incomes, credit, and the middle class.[^cite_note-russiaprofile-99][^cite_note-challenges_of_medv_era-100] [A fund for oil revenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stabilization_Fund_of_the_Russian_Federation) allowed Russia to repay the Soviet Union's debts by 2005. Russia joined the [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization) in August 2012.[^cite_note-101]

In 2006, Putin launched an industry consolidation programme to bring the main aircraft-producing companies under a single umbrella organization, the [United Aircraft Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Aircraft_Corporation) (UAC).[^cite_note-102][^cite_note-ato1-103] In September 2020, the UAC general director announced that the UAC will receive the largest-ever post-Soviet government support package for the aircraft industry in order to pay and renegotiate the debt.[^cite_note-104][^cite_note-105]



Putin, [Gazprom](320489.md) CEO [Alexey Miller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexey_Miller), [CNPC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_National_Petroleum_Corporation) General Manager [Zhou Jiping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhou_Jiping), and Chinese President [Xi Jinping](2017814.md). The Russian economy is heavily dependent on the [export of natural resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_policy_of_Russia) such as oil and natural gas.

[^cite_note-106]

In 2014, Putin signed a deal to supply China with 38 billion cubic meters of natural gas per year. [Power of Siberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_of_Siberia), which Putin has called the "world's biggest construction project", was launched in 2019 and is expected to continue for 30 years at an ultimate cost to China of \$400bn.[^cite_note-107] The [ongoing financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_financial_crisis_\(2014%E2%80%93present\)) began in the second half of 2014 when the Russian ruble collapsed due to a decline in the price of oil and [international sanctions against Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_sanctions_during_the_Ukrainian_crisis). These events in turn led to loss of investor confidence and capital flight, although it has also been argued that the sanctions had little to no effect on Russia's economy.[^cite_note-108][^cite_note-109][^cite_note-110] In 2014, the [Organized Crime and Corruption Reporting Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organized_Crime_and_Corruption_Reporting_Project) named Putin their Person of the Year for furthering corruption and organized crime.[^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112]

According to *[Meduza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meduza_\(website\))*, Putin has since 2007 predicted on several occasions that Russia will become one of the world's five largest economies. In 2013, he said Russia was one of the five biggest economies in terms of gross domestic product but still lagged behind other countries on indicators such as labour productivity.[^cite_note-113] By the end of 2023, Putin planned to spend almost 40% of [public expenditures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_budget_of_Russia) on defense and security.[^cite_note-114]

### Environmental policy

Main articles: [Environment of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environment_of_Russia), [Environmental issues in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_issues_in_Russia), and [Climate change in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Russia)

In 2004, Putin signed the [Kyoto Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyoto_Protocol) treaty designed to reduce greenhouse gas emissions.[^cite_note-115] However, Russia did not face mandatory cuts, because the Kyoto Protocol limits emissions to a percentage increase or decrease from 1990 levels and Russia's greenhouse-gas emissions fell well below the 1990 baseline due to a drop in economic output after the breakup of the Soviet Union,[^cite_note-116] excluding emissions from [land use, land-use change and forestry (LULUCF)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_use,_land-use_change,_and_forestry).[^cite_note-117]

In 2019, Russia joined the [Paris Agreement](30242372.md).[^cite_note-118] Russia's goal is to reach [net zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Net_zero) by 2060, but [its energy strategy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_policy_of_Russia) to 2035 is mostly about burning more [fossil fuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuels).[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120] Reporting military emissions is voluntary and, as of 2024, no data is available since before the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md).[^cite_note-121]

Putin described [climate change](5042951.md) as a concerning fact with big consequences for Russia. Though he expressed doubt that climate change was man-made, he stated that Russia will try to reduce man-made emissions with forests and "low-emission energy", such as [natural gas](42827258.md), [nuclear energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_power_in_Russia), and [hydroenergy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroenergy_in_Russia). He also argued that rich countries should provide finance and technology to those with less money for lower emissions.[^cite_note-122] Energy expert Tatiana Lanshina described his policy as "mimicry of climate policy", accusing him of turning environmentalism into a tool of political influence.[^cite_note-123]

### Religious policy

Main article: [Religion in Russia](3060242.md)



Putin with religious leaders of Russia, February 2001

Putin regularly attends the most important services of the [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church) on the main holy days and has established a good relationship with [Patriarchs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarchs_of_Moscow) of the Russian Church, the late [Alexy II of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexy_II_of_Moscow) and the current [Kirill of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirill_of_Moscow). As president, Putin took an active personal part in promoting the [Act of Canonical Communion with the Moscow Patriarchate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_of_Canonical_Communion_with_the_Moscow_Patriarchate), signed 17 May 2007, which restored relations between the Moscow-based Russian Orthodox Church and the [Russian Orthodox Church Outside Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church_Outside_Russia) after the 80-year schism.[^cite_note-124]

Under Putin, the [Hasidic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasidic) [Federation of Jewish Communities of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_of_Jewish_Communities_of_Russia) became increasingly influential within the Jewish community, partly due to the influence of Federation-supporting businessmen mediated through their alliances with Putin, notably [Lev Leviev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lev_Avnerovich_Leviev) and [Roman Abramovich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Abramovich).[^cite_note-Russia-125][^cite_note-126] According to the [Jewish Telegraphic Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Telegraphic_Agency), Putin is popular amongst the [Russian Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Jewish) community, who see him as a force for stability. Russia's [chief rabbi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Rabbi_of_Russia), [Berel Lazar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berel_Lazar), said Putin "paid great attention to the needs of our community and related to us with a deep respect".[^cite_note-127] In 2016, [Ronald S. Lauder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronald_S._Lauder), the president of the [World Jewish Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Jewish_Congress), also praised Putin for making Russia "a country where Jews are welcome".[^cite_note-wjcrussiafight-128]

Human rights organizations and religious freedom advocates have criticized the state of religious freedom in Russia.[^cite_note-WaPost-2016-129] In 2016, Putin oversaw the passage of legislation that prohibited missionary activity in Russia.[^cite_note-WaPost-2016-129] Nonviolent religious minority groups have been repressed under anti-extremism laws, especially [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah's_Witnesses).[^cite_note-130] One of the [2020 amendments to the Constitution of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_amendments_to_the_Constitution_of_Russia) directly refers to [belief in God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_references_to_God).[^cite_note-131]

### Military development

Main article: [2008 Russian military reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Russian_military_reform)



Putin with Russia's long-serving Defense Minister [Sergei Shoigu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergei_Shoigu) (left) and Chief of the General Staff [Valery Gerasimov](37587470.md) at the [Vostok 2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vostok_2018) military exercise

The resumption of long-distance flights of Russia's [strategic bombers](214209.md) was followed by the announcement by Russian defense minister [Anatoliy Serdyukov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatoliy_Serdyukov) during his meeting with Putin on 5 December 2007, that 11 ships, including the aircraft carrier *[Kuznetsov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_aircraft_carrier_Admiral_Kuznetsov)*, would take part in the first major navy sortie into the Mediterranean since Soviet times.[^cite_note-132][^cite_note-133]

Key elements of the reform included reducing the armed forces to a strength of one million, reducing the number of officers, centralizing officer training from 65 military schools into 10 systemic military training centers, creating a professional [NCO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-commissioned_officer) corps, reducing the size of the central command, introducing more civilian logistics and auxiliary staff, elimination of cadre-strength formations, reorganizing the reserves, reorganizing the army into a brigade system, and reorganizing air forces into an airbase system instead of regiments.[^cite_note-134]



Russian postage stamp honoring a soldier killed in the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md)

According to the Kremlin, Putin embarked on a build-up of [Russia's nuclear capabilities](588941.md#Nuclear_weapons) because of U.S. president [George W. Bush](3414021.md)'s unilateral decision to withdraw from the 1972 [Anti-Ballistic Missile Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Ballistic_Missile_Treaty).[^cite_note-135] To counter what Putin sees as the United States' goal of undermining Russia's strategic nuclear deterrent, Moscow has embarked on a program to develop new weapons capable of defeating any new American ballistic missile defense or interception system. Some analysts believe that this nuclear strategy under Putin has brought Russia into violation of the 1987 [Intermediate-Range Nuclear Forces Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intermediate-Range_Nuclear_Forces_Treaty).[^cite_note-Hurlbert-2018-136]

Accordingly, U.S. President [Donald Trump](4848272.md) announced the U.S. would no longer consider itself bound by the treaty's provisions, raising nuclear tensions between the two powers.[^cite_note-Hurlbert-2018-136] This prompted Putin to state that Russia would not launch first in a nuclear conflict but that "an aggressor should know that vengeance is inevitable, that he will be annihilated, and we would be the victims of the aggression. We will go to heaven as martyrs".[^cite_note-137]

Putin has also sought to increase Russian territorial claims in the Arctic and its military presence there. In August 2007, Russian expedition [Arktika 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arktika_2007), part of research related to the [2001 Russian territorial extension claim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_shelf_of_Russia#2001_extension_claim), planted a flag on the seabed at the North Pole.[^cite_note-pole_flag-138] Both Russian submarines and troops deployed in the Arctic have been increasing.[^cite_note-Military_buildup1-139][^cite_note-Military_buildup2-140]

### Human rights policy

Main article: [Human rights in Russia](785595.md)

See also: [Dima Yakovlev Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dima_Yakovlev_Law), [Russian foreign agent law](39572380.md), and [Russian Internet Restriction Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Internet_Restriction_Bill)



[Russian opposition](38212364.md) politician [Alexei Navalny](29174356.md) attends a march in memory of [assassinated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Boris_Nemtsov) opposition politician [Boris Nemtsov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Nemtsov), Moscow, 29 February 2020

New York City–based NGO [Human Rights Watch](46530.md), in a report titled *Laws of Attrition*, authored by Hugh Williamson, the British director of HRW's Europe & Central Asia Division, has claimed that since May 2012, when Putin was reelected as president, Russia has enacted many restrictive laws, started inspections of non-governmental organizations, harassed, intimidated and imprisoned political activists, and started to restrict critics. The new laws include the "foreign agents" law, which is widely regarded as overbroad by including Russian human rights organizations that receive some international grant funding, the treason law, and the assembly law, which penalizes many expressions of dissent.[^cite_note-141][^cite_note-142] Human rights activists have criticized Russia for censoring speech of LGBT activists due to ["the gay propaganda law"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_gay_propaganda_law)[^cite_note-143] and increasing violence against LGBT+ people due to the law.[^cite_note-144][^cite_note-145][^cite_note-146]

In 2020, Putin signed a law on labelling individuals and organizations receiving funding from abroad as "foreign agents". The law is an expansion of "foreign agent" legislation adopted in 2012.[^cite_note-147][^cite_note-148]

As of June 2020, per the Memorial Human Rights Center, there were 380 political prisoners in Russia, including 63 individuals prosecuted, directly or indirectly, for political activities (including Alexey Navalny) and 245 prosecuted for their involvement with one of the Muslim organizations that are banned in Russia. 78 individuals on the list, i.e., more than 20% of the total, are residents of Crimea.[^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150] As of December 2022, more than 4,000 people were prosecuted for criticizing the war in Ukraine under Russia's [war censorship laws](70646883.md).[^cite_note-151]

### Media

See also: [Mass media in Russia](15861222.md), [Media freedom in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_freedom_in_Russia), and [Propaganda in Russia](45060755.md)



Putin [being interviewed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tucker_Carlson's_interview_with_Vladimir_Putin) by [Tucker Carlson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tucker_Carlson) on 6 February 2024

Scott Gehlbach, a professor of Political Science at the [University of Wisconsin–Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Wisconsin%E2%80%93Madison), has claimed that since 1999, Putin has systematically punished journalists who challenge his official point of view.[^cite_note-152] [Maria Lipman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maria_Lipman), an American writing in *[Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Affairs)* claims, "The crackdown that followed Putin's return to the Kremlin in 2012 extended to the liberal media, which had until then been allowed to operate fairly independently".[^cite_note-153] The Internet has attracted Putin's attention because his critics have tried to use it to challenge his control of information.[^cite_note-154] Marian K. Leighton, who worked for the [CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA) as a Soviet analyst in the 1980s, says, "Having muzzled Russia's print and broadcast media, Putin focused his energies on the Internet."[^cite_note-155]

Robert W. Orttung and Christopher Walker reported that "[Reporters Without Borders](526054.md), for instance, ranked Russia 148 in its 2013 list of 179 countries in terms of freedom of the press. It particularly criticized Russia for the crackdown on the political opposition and the failure of the authorities to vigorously pursue and bring to justice criminals who have murdered journalists. [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House) ranks Russian media as "not free", indicating that basic safeguards and guarantees for journalists and media enterprises are absent.[^cite_note-156] About two-thirds of Russians use television as their primary source of daily news,[^cite_note-157] while around 85% of Russians get most of their information from Russian state media.[^cite_note-Time-Stengel-158]

In the early 2000s, Putin and his circle began promoting the idea in Russian media that they are the modern-day version of the 17th-century [Romanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Romanov) tsars who ended Russia's "[Time of Troubles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_of_Troubles)", meaning they claim to be the peacemakers and stabilizers after the fall of the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-159] Since the 2022 Ukraine invasion, Putin has [only once](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tucker_Carlson's_interview_with_Vladimir_Putin) granted an interview to a Western journalist, namely [Tucker Carlson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tucker_Carlson) in February 2024.[^cite_note-NYTimesInfo-160]
