Putin attends the [Orthodox Christmas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christmas_in_Russia) service in the village of Turginovo in [Kalininsky District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalininsky_District,_Tver_Oblast), Tver Oblast, 7 January 2016

Putin has promoted explicitly conservative policies in social, cultural, and political matters, both at home and abroad. Putin has attacked [globalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalism) and [neoliberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoliberalism) and is identified by scholars with [Russian conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_conservatism).[^cite_note-conservatism-161] Putin has promoted new think tanks that bring together like-minded intellectuals and writers. For example, the Izborsky Club, founded in 2012 by the conservative right-wing journalist [Alexander Prokhanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Prokhanov), stresses (i) Russian nationalism, (ii) the restoration of Russia's historical greatness, and (iii) systematic opposition to liberal ideas and policies.[^cite_note-162] [Vladislav Surkov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladislav_Surkov), a senior government official, has been one of the key economic consultants during Putin's presidency.[^cite_note-163] [Leonid Bershidsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonid_Bershidsky) analyzed Putin's interview with the *[Financial Times](136566.md)* and concluded, "Putin is an imperialist of the old Soviet school, rather than a nationalist or a racist, and he has cooperated with, and promoted, people who are known to be gay".[^cite_note-164]

In cultural and social affairs, Putin has collaborated closely with the [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church). [Patriarch Kirill of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarch_Kirill_of_Moscow), head of the Church, endorsed his election in 2012 stating Putin's terms were like "a miracle of God".[^cite_note-165] Steven Myers reports, "The church, once heavily repressed, had emerged from the Soviet collapse as one of the most respected ... Now Kiril led the faithful directly into an alliance with the state".[^cite_note-166]

Mark Woods, a [Baptist Union of Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptists_Together) minister and contributing editor to *Christian Today*, provides specific examples of how the Church has backed the expansion of Russian power into Crimea and eastern Ukraine.[^cite_note-167] Some Russian Orthodox believers consider Putin a corrupt and brutal strongman or even a tyrant. Others do not admire him but appreciate that he aggravates their political opponents. Still others appreciate that Putin defends some although not all Orthodox teachings, whether he believes in them himself.[^cite_note-168]

On abortion, Putin stated: "In the modern world, the decision is up to the woman herself".[^cite_note-169] This put him at odds with the [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church).[^cite_note-170] In 2020, he supported efforts to reduce the number of abortions instead of prohibiting it.[^cite_note-171] On 28 November 2023, during a speech to the [World Russian People's Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Russian_People's_Council), Putin urged Russian women to have "seven, eight, or even more children" and said "large families must become the norm, a way of life for all of Russia's people".[^cite_note-172]

Putin supported the [2020 Russian constitutional referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Russian_constitutional_referendum), which passed and defined marriage as a relationship between one man and one woman in the [Constitution of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Russia).[^cite_note-Times-3Mar20-173][^cite_note-Guardian-2Mar20-174][^cite_note-MosTimes-2Mar20-175]

### International sporting events



Putin, [FIFA](11049.md) president [Gianni Infantino](9286117.md), French president [Emmanuel Macron](43671127.md) and Croatian former president [Kolinda Grabar-Kitarović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kolinda_Grabar-Kitarovi%C4%87) at the [2018 FIFA World Cup Final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_FIFA_World_Cup_Final) in Russia, as French forward [Kylian Mbappé](48711857.md) receives the best young player award

In 2007, Putin led a successful effort on behalf of [Sochi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sochi) for the [2014 Winter Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Winter_Olympics) and the [2014 Winter Paralympics](2311504.md),[^cite_note-kremlin-2014-176] the first [Winter Olympic Games](34069.md) to ever be hosted by Russia. In 2008, the city of [Kazan](202295.md) won the bid for the [2013 Summer Universiade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Summer_Universiade); on 2 December 2010, Russia won the right to host the [2017 FIFA Confederations Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_FIFA_Confederations_Cup) and [2018 FIFA World Cup](13327177.md), also for the first time in Russian history. In 2013, Putin stated that gay athletes would not face any discrimination at the 2014 Sochi Winter Olympics.[^cite_note-177]

## Foreign policy

Main article: [Foreign policy of Vladimir Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_Vladimir_Putin)

See also: [Foreign relations of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Russia) and [List of international presidential trips made by Vladimir Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_international_presidential_trips_made_by_Vladimir_Putin)



Putin together with North Korean leader [Kim Jong Un](5962511.md), Chinese leader [Xi Jinping](2017814.md), and Pakistani Prime Minister [Shehbaz Sharif](6502561.md) during the [2025 China Victory Day Parade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_China_Victory_Day_Parade)



Putin's visit to the United States, November 2001

Generally, Putin's tenure experienced tensions with the West,[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179] as well as stronger relations with China.[^cite_note-\:82-180]<sup>: 499 </sup> Anna Borshchevskaya, in her 2022 book, summarizes Putin's main foreign policy objectives as originating in his 30 December 1999 document, which appeared on the government's website, "Russia at the Turn of the Millennium".[^cite_note-181] She presents Putin as orienting himself to the plan that "Russia is a country with unique values in danger of losing its unity – which... is a historic Russian fear. This again points to the fundamental issue of Russia's identity issues – and how the state had manipulated these to drive anti-Western security narratives to erode the US-led global order... Moreover, a look at Russia's distribution of forces over the years under Putin has been heavily weighted towards the south (Syria, Ukraine, Middle East), another indicator of the Kremlin's threat perceptions".[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183]

Putin spoke favorably of [artificial intelligence](1164.md) regarding foreign policy, "Artificial intelligence is the future, not only for Russia, but for all humankind. It comes with colossal opportunities, but also threats that are difficult to predict. Whoever becomes the leader in this sphere will become the ruler of the world".[^cite_note-Hoover_Institution-184]

### Asia-Pacific

See also: [India–Russia relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%E2%80%93Russia_relations) and [Shanghai Cooperation Organisation](152422.md)

Putin with Indian Prime Minister Modi in New Delhi

Putin with Indian Prime Minister [Narendra Modi](444222.md) in New Delhi, India, 5 December 2025

In 2012, Putin wrote an article in Indian newspaper *[The Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hindu)*, saying: "The Declaration on Strategic Partnership between India and Russia signed in October 2000 became a truly historic step".[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-bbc.co.uk-186] India remains the largest customer of Russian military equipment, and the two countries share a historically strong [strategic and diplomatic relationship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%E2%80%93Russia_relations).[^cite_note-187] In October 2022, Putin described India and China as "close allies and partners".[^cite_note-188] Under Putin, Russia has maintained positive relations with the Asian states of [SCO](152422.md) and [BRICS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BRICS), which include China, India, Pakistan, and post-Soviet states of Central Asia.[^cite_note-189][^cite_note-190]

Putin and Prime Minister [Shinzo Abe](2634434.md) frequently met each other to discuss the Japan–Russia territorial disputes. Putin also voiced his willingness to construct a rail bridge between the two countries.[^cite_note-191] Despite numerous meetings, no agreement was signed before Abe's resignation in 2020.[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193] Putin also made the first Russian or Soviet leader to visit North Korea, meeting [Kim Jong Il](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Jong_Il) in July 2000, shortly after a visit to South Korea.[^cite_note-194]



Putin with Chinese president [Xi Jinping](2017814.md) and other leaders at the [Shanghai Cooperation Organisation](152422.md) summit in Uzbekistan on 16 September 2022

Putin made three visits to Mongolia and has enjoyed good relations with its neighbor. Putin and his Mongolian counterpart signed a permanent treaty on friendship between the two states in September 2019, further enhancing trade and cultural exchanges.[^cite_note-195][^cite_note-196] Putin became the first Russian or Soviet leader to visit Indonesia in half a century in 2007, resulting in the signing of an arms deal.[^cite_note-197] In another visit, Putin commented on long-standing ties and friendship between Russia and Indonesia.[^cite_note-198] Russia has also boosted relations with Vietnam after 2011,[^cite_note-199] and with Afghanistan in the 2010s, giving military and economic aid.[^cite_note-200][^cite_note-201] The relations between Russia and the Philippines received a boost in 2016 as Putin forged closer bilateral ties with his Filipino counterpart, [Rodrigo Duterte](7407293.md).[^cite_note-202][^cite_note-203] Putin has good relations with Malaysia and its then Prime Minister [Mahathir Mohamad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahathir_Mohamad).[^cite_note-204] Putin criticized violence in Myanmar against the Rohingya minorities in 2017.[^cite_note-205] Following the [2021 Myanmar coup d'état](66567997.md), Russia has pledged to boost ties with the Myanmar military regime.[^cite_note-206]

In September 2007, Putin attended the [APEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/APEC) meeting [held in Sydney, Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/APEC_Australia_2007), where he met with Prime Minister [John Howard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Howard) and signed a uranium trade deal for Australia to sell uranium to Russia. This was the first visit by a Russian president to Australia.[^cite_note-207] Putin again visited Australia for [2014 G20 Brisbane summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_G20_Brisbane_summit). The [Abbott government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbott_government) denounced Putin's use of military force in Ukraine in 2014 as "bullying" and "utterly unacceptable".[^cite_note-208] Amid calls to ban Putin from attending the 2014 G20 Summit, Prime Minister [Tony Abbott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Abbott) said he would "shirtfront" (challenge) the Russian leader over the [shooting down of MH17](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysia_Airlines_Flight_17) by Russian-backed rebels, which had killed 38 Australians.[^cite_note-209] Putin denied responsibility for the killings.[^cite_note-210]

### China

See also: [China–Russia relations](30964882.md)



Putin with Chinese President [Xi Jinping](2017814.md) and Mongolian President [Ukhnaagiin Khürelsükh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukhnaagiin_Kh%C3%BCrels%C3%BCkh) in Beijing, China, 2 September 2025

In the 21st century, [Sino-Russian relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Russian_relations_since_1991) have significantly strengthened bilaterally and economically—the [Treaty of Friendship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_Sino-Russian_Treaty_of_Friendship), and the construction of the [ESPO oil pipeline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Siberia%E2%80%93Pacific_Ocean_oil_pipeline) and the [Power of Siberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_of_Siberia) gas pipeline formed a "special relationship" between the two [great powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_power).[^cite_note-211] Putin and Chinese leader [Hu Jintao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu_Jintao) held their first meeting in December 2002. The two leaders met regularly, meeting face to face five or six times a year. Russian Prime Minister Dimitry Medvedev and Chinese Premier [Wen Jiabao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wen_Jiabao) also met regularly, with Wen quipping in 2007 that "We didn't even use prepared speeches."[^cite_note-\:82-180]<sup>: 497 </sup> China backed Russia in the [Second Chechen War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Chechen_War) and regards to Russia's concerns of NATO expansion, while Russia backed China regarding the issues of Taiwan, [Tibet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibet_Autonomous_Region) and [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang). The two countries also increasingly cooperated in the [United Nations Security Council](31956.md).[^cite_note-\:82-180]<sup>: 499 </sup>

On the eve of a 2013 [state visit](327283.md) to Moscow by Chinese leader [Xi Jinping](2017814.md), Putin remarked that the two nations were forging a special relationship.[^cite_note-212] Xi visited the Operational Command Headquarters of the [Russian Armed Forces](25709.md), the first time a foreign leader visited the building.[^cite_note-\:82-180]<sup>: 506–507 </sup> Ties have continued to deepen after the 2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine, with Russia increasingly becoming dependent on China while it is under [large-scale international sanctions](70323386.md).[^cite_note-\:0-213]

### Post-Soviet states

Further information: [Colour revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colour_revolution), [Russia–Ukraine relations](17446303.md), [Belarus–Russia relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belarus%E2%80%93Russia_relations), [Georgia–Russia relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia%E2%80%93Russia_relations), [Armenia–Russia relations](13875647.md), [Azerbaijan–Russia relations](17444565.md), [Kyrgyzstan–Russia relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyrgyzstan%E2%80%93Russia_relations), [Kazakhstan–Russia relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhstan%E2%80%93Russia_relations), and [Eurasian Economic Union](27290106.md)

See also: [Commonwealth of Independent States](36870.md)



[Post-Soviet states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Soviet_states) in English alphabetical order:&#x20;

1. Armenia

2. Azerbaijan

3. Belarus

4. Estonia

5. Georgia

6. Kazakhstan

7. Kyrgyzstan

8. Latvia

9. Lithuania

10. Moldova

11. Russia

12. Tajikistan

13. Turkmenistan

14. Ukraine

15. Uzbekistan

Under Putin, the Kremlin has consistently stated that Russia has a [sphere of influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sphere_of_influence) and "privileged interests" over other [post-Soviet states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Soviet_states), which are referred to as the "near abroad" in Russia. It has also been stated that the post-Soviet states are strategically vital to [Russian interests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Russia).[^cite_note-214] Some Russia experts have compared this concept to the [Monroe Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monroe_Doctrine).[^cite_note-215]

A series of so-called [colour revolutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colour_revolution) in the [post-Soviet states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Soviet_states), namely the [Rose Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rose_Revolution) in Georgia in 2003, the [Orange Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_Revolution) in Ukraine in 2004, and the [Tulip Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulip_Revolution) in Kyrgyzstan in 2005, led to frictions in the relations of those countries with Russia. In December 2004, Putin criticized the Rose and Orange revolutions, saying: "If you have permanent revolutions, you risk plunging the post-Soviet space into endless conflict."[^cite_note-blueandorange-216]

Putin allegedly declared at a NATO-Russia summit in 2008 that if Ukraine joined NATO, Russia could contend to annex the [Ukrainian East](13065940.md) and [Crimea](163045.md).[^cite_note-217] At the summit, he told U.S. President [George W. Bush](3414021.md) that "Ukraine is not even a state!", while the following year, Putin referred to Ukraine as "[Little Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Russia)".[^cite_note-218] Following the [Revolution of Dignity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_Dignity) in March 2014, [the Russian Federation annexed Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annexation_of_Crimea_by_the_Russian_Federation).[^cite_note-walker-the-guardian-2014-descend-219][^cite_note-bloomberg-news-2014-request-220][^cite_note-Radyuhin-221] According to Putin, this was done because "Crimea has always been and remains an inseparable part of Russia."[^cite_note-222]



Putin hosted a meeting of the Russian-led military alliance, the [Collective Security Treaty Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_Security_Treaty_Organization) (CSTO), in Moscow on 16 May 2022.

After the Russian annexation of Crimea, he said that Ukraine includes "regions of Russia's historic south" and "was created on a whim by the [Bolsheviks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsheviks)".[^cite_note-cbsnews.com-223] He went on to declare that the [February 2014 ousting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_Dignity) of [Ukrainian President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_President) [Viktor Yanukovych](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktor_Yanukovych) had been orchestrated by the West as an attempt to weaken Russia.[^cite_note-cbsnews.com-223] In a July 2014 speech during the Russian-supported [armed insurgency in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Donbas_\(2014%E2%80%932022\)) [Eastern Ukraine](13065940.md), Putin stated he would use Russia's "entire arsenal of available means" up to "operations under international humanitarian law and the right of self-defense" to protect [Russian speakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_speakers) outside Russia.[^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225]

In late August 2014, Putin stated: "People who have their own views on history and the [history of our country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Russia) may argue with me, but it seems to me that the Russian and [Ukrainian peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_people) are practically one people."[^cite_note-226] After making a similar statement, in late December 2015, he stated: "the [Ukrainian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_culture), as well as [Ukrainian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_literature), surely has a source of its own".[^cite_note-227] In July 2021, he published a lengthy article *[On the Historical Unity of Russians and Ukrainians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Historical_Unity_of_Russians_and_Ukrainians)*[^cite_note-228] revisiting these themes, and saying the formation of a Ukrainian state hostile to Moscow was "comparable in its consequences to the use of weapons of mass destruction against us"[^cite_note-229][^cite_note-230]—it was made mandatory reading for military-political training in the Russian Armed Forces.[^cite_note-231]

During the [Russo-Ukrainian war](70149799.md), Putin set out several conditions for a [peace agreement](70255183.md), which included permanent Ukrainian neutrality, limits on the size of Ukraine's military, and the cession of four southeastern regions [annexed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_annexation_of_Donetsk,_Kherson,_Luhansk_and_Zaporizhzhia_oblasts) by Russia in 2022 but not fully [controlled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian-occupied_territories_of_Ukraine) by Russian forces.[^cite_note-232] Ukraine rejected the terms, which Western officials and analysts said would effectively compromise the country's sovereignty and independence.[^cite_note-233]



Ukrainian president [Volodymyr Zelenskyy](59210523.md), German chancellor [Angela Merkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angela_Merkel), French president [Emmanuel Macron](43671127.md), and Putin met in Paris on 9 December 2019 in the "[Normandy Format](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normandy_Format)" aimed at ending the [war in Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Donbas_\(2014%E2%80%932022\)).

In August 2008, [Georgian President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Georgia) [Mikheil Saakashvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikheil_Saakashvili) attempted to restore control over the breakaway South Ossetia. However, the Georgian military was soon defeated in the resulting [2008 South Ossetia War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Georgian_War) after regular Russian forces entered South Ossetia and then other parts of Georgia, also opening a second front in the other Georgian breakaway province of Abkhazia with Abkhazian forces.[^cite_note-234]

Despite existing or past tensions between Russia and most of the post-Soviet states, Putin has followed the policy of Eurasian integration. Putin endorsed the idea of a [Eurasian Union](27290106.md) in 2011;[^cite_note-235][^cite_note-yahoo-reuters-236] the concept was proposed by the [president of Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Kazakhstan) in 1994.[^cite_note-237] On 18 November 2011, the presidents of Belarus, Kazakhstan, and Russia signed an agreement setting a target of establishing the Eurasian Union by 2015.[^cite_note-bbc18Nov2011-238] The Eurasian Union was established on 1 January 2015.[^cite_note-239]

Under Putin, Russia's relations have improved significantly with Uzbekistan, the second-largest post-Soviet republic after Ukraine. This was demonstrated in Putin's visit to [Tashkent](57533.md) in May 2000, after lukewarm relations under Yeltsin and [Islam Karimov](335230.md), who had long distanced itself from Moscow.[^cite_note-240] In another meeting in 2014, Russia agreed to write off Uzbek debt.[^cite_note-241] A theme of a greater Soviet region, including the former USSR and many of its neighbors or imperial-era states—rather than just post-Soviet Russia—has been consistent in Putin's May Day speeches.[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244]

### United States, the West, and NATO

See also: [Anti-American sentiment in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-American_sentiment_in_Russia), [Russia–NATO relations](19066574.md), and [Russia–United States relations](4482509.md)

Under Putin, Russia's relationships with NATO and the U.S. have passed through several stages. When he first became president, relations were cautious, but after the [9/11 attacks](5058690.md), Putin quickly supported the U.S. in the [war on terror](13425800.md), and the opportunity for partnership appeared.[^cite_note-Bi-Partisan-245] According to Russian studies scholar [Stephen F. Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_F._Cohen), the U.S. "repaid ... by further expanding [NATO](21133.md) to Russia's borders and by unilaterally withdrawing from the 1972 [Anti-Ballistic Missile Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Ballistic_Missile_Treaty)",[^cite_note-Bi-Partisan-245] but others pointed out the applications from [new countries willing to join NATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enlargement_of_NATO) was driven primarily by Russian behavior in [Chechnya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Chechen_War), [Transnistria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transnistria_War), [Abkhazia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Abkhazia_\(1992%E2%80%931993\)), the [1991 Soviet coup attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Soviet_coup_attempt), as well as calls to restore USSR in its previous borders by prominent Russian politicians.[^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247]

From 2003, when Russia strongly opposed the U.S. when it waged the [Iraq War](5043324.md), Putin became ever more distant from the West, and relations steadily deteriorated. According to Cohen, the narrative of the mainstream U.S. media, following that of the [White House](33057.md), became anti-Putin.[^cite_note-Bi-Partisan-245] In an interview with [Michael Stürmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_St%C3%BCrmer), Putin said there were three questions which most concerned Russia and Eastern Europe: namely, the status of Kosovo, the [Treaty on Conventional Armed Forces in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_on_Conventional_Armed_Forces_in_Europe) and American plans to build missile defence sites in Poland and the Czech Republic, and suggested that all three were linked.[^cite_note-Sturmer-248] His view was that concessions by the West on one of the questions might be met with concessions from Russia on another.[^cite_note-Sturmer-248]

In 2003, [relations between Russia and the United Kingdom](12342392.md) deteriorated when the United Kingdom granted political asylum to Putin's former patron, [oligarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_oligarch) [Boris Berezovsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Berezovsky_\(businessman\)).[^cite_note-expul-249] This deterioration was intensified by allegations that the British were spying and making secret payments to pro-democracy and human rights groups.[^cite_note-spy-rock-250] The end of 2006 brought more strained relations in the wake of the death by [polonium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polonium) poisoning in London of former KGB and [FSB](385990.md) officer [Alexander Litvinenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Litvinenko), who became an [MI6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secret_Intelligence_Service) agent in 2003. In 2007, the crisis in relations continued with the expulsion of four Russian [envoys](82433.md) over Russia's refusal to extradite former KGB bodyguard [Andrei Lugovoi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrei_Lugovoi) to face charges over the murder.[^cite_note-expul-249] Mirroring the British actions, Russia expelled UK diplomats and took other retaliatory steps.[^cite_note-expul-249] In 2015, the British Government launched a [public inquiry](826604.md) into Litvinenko's death, presided over by [Robert Owen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Owen_\(judge\)), a former British High Court judge.[^cite_note-251] The Owen report, published on 21 January 2016, stated, "The FSB operation to kill Mr. Litvinenko was probably approved by Mr [Patrushev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolai_Patrushev) and also by President Putin".[^cite_note-252] The report outlined some possible motives for the murder, including Litvinenko's public statements and [books](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blowing_Up_Russia) about [the alleged involvement of the FSB in mass murder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_apartment_bombings), and what was "undoubtedly a personal dimension to the antagonism" between Putin and Litvinenko.[^cite_note-253]

> One single center of power. One single center of force. One single center of decision-making. This is the world of one master, one sovereign. ... Primarily, the United States has overstepped its national borders, and in every area.

— Putin criticizing the United States in his [Munich Speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_speech_of_Vladimir_Putin), 2007

[^cite_note-254]

In a January 2007 interview, Putin said Russia was in favor of a democratic [multipolar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polarity_in_international_relations) world and strengthening the systems of [international law](8195726.md).[^cite_note-255] In February 2007, Putin criticized what he called the United States' monopolistic dominance in global relations, and "almost uncontained hyper use of force in international relations". He said the result of it is that "no one feels safe! Because no one can feel that international law is like a stone wall that will protect them. Of course such a policy stimulates an arms race."[^cite_note-Munich-256] This came to be known as the [Munich Speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_speech_of_Vladimir_Putin), and NATO secretary [Jaap de Hoop Scheffer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaap_de_Hoop_Scheffer) called the speech "disappointing and not helpful".[^cite_note-BBC-257]

The months following Putin's Munich Speech[^cite_note-Munich-256] were marked by tension and a surge in rhetoric on both sides of the Atlantic. Both Russian and American officials, however, denied the idea of a [new Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Cold_War).[^cite_note-258] Putin publicly opposed plans for the [U.S. missile shield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_missile_defense) in Europe and presented President [George W. Bush](3414021.md) with a counterproposal on 7 June 2007, which was declined.[^cite_note-259] Russia suspended its participation in the Conventional Forces in Europe treaty on 11 December 2007.[^cite_note-260]

Putin opposed Kosovo's unilateral [declaration of independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Kosovo_declaration_of_independence) from Serbia on 17 February 2008, warning that it would destabilize the whole system of international relations.[^cite_note-261] He described the [recognition of Kosovo's independence](15808914.md) by several major world powers as "a terrible precedent, which will de facto blow apart the whole system of international relations, developed not over decades, but over centuries", and that "they have not thought through the results of what they are doing. At the end of the day it is a two-ended stick and the second end will come back and hit them in the face".[^cite_note-262] In March 2014, Putin used Kosovo's declaration of independence as a justification for recognizing [the independence of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_Independence_of_the_Republic_of_Crimea), citing the so-called "[Kosovo independence precedent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosovo_independence_precedent)".[^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264]

After the 9/11 attacks on the U.S. in 2001, Putin had good relations with the American President [George W. Bush](3414021.md), and many Western European leaders. His "cooler" and "more business-like" relationship with German chancellor [Angela Merkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angela_Merkel) is often attributed to Merkel's upbringing in the former [DDR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Germany), where Putin was stationed as a KGB agent.[^cite_note-265] He had a very friendly and warm relationship with Prime Minister of Italy [Silvio Berlusconi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silvio_Berlusconi);[^cite_note-266] the two leaders often described their relationship as a close friendship, continuing to organize bilateral meetings even after [Berlusconi's resignation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resignation_of_Silvio_Berlusconi) in November 2011.[^cite_note-267] When [Berlusconi died](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Silvio_Berlusconi) in 2023, Putin described him as an "extraordinary man" and a "true friend".[^cite_note-268][^cite_note-269]

The NATO-led [military intervention in Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_military_intervention_in_Libya) in 2011 prompted a widespread wave of criticism from several world leaders, including Putin, who said that the [United Nations Security Council Resolution 1973](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_1973) is "defective and flawed", adding: "It allows everything. It resembles medieval calls for crusades".[^cite_note-270]

In late 2013, Russian-American relations deteriorated further when the United States canceled a summit for the first time since 1960 after Putin gave asylum to the American [Edward Snowden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Snowden), who had leaked massive amounts of classified information from the NSA.[^cite_note-Shuster-271][^cite_note-272] In 2014, Russia was suspended from the [G8](59755.md) group as a result of its [annexation of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annexation_of_Crimea_by_the_Russian_Federation).[^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274] Putin gave a speech highly critical of the United States, accusing them of destabilizing world order and trying to "reshape the world" to its own benefit.[^cite_note-275] In June 2015, Putin said that Russia has no intention of attacking NATO.[^cite_note-276]

&#x20;On 9 November 2016, Putin congratulated [Donald Trump](4848272.md) on becoming the 45th president of the United States.[^cite_note-277] In December 2016, US intelligence officials (headed by [James Clapper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Clapper)) quoted by [CBS News](359551.md) stated that Putin approved the [email hacking and cyber attacks during the U.S. election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_election_interference_by_Russia), against the Democratic presidential nominee, [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton). A spokesman for Putin denied the reports.[^cite_note-278] Putin has repeatedly accused Hillary Clinton, who served as U.S. secretary of state from 2009 to 2013, of interfering in [Russia's internal affairs](25704.md),[^cite_note-279] and in December 2016, Clinton accused Putin of having a personal grudge against her.[^cite_note-280][^cite_note-281] Putin stated that U.S.–Russian relations, already at the lowest level since the end of the [Cold War](325329.md),[^cite_note-282] continued to deteriorate after Trump took office in January 2017.[^cite_note-283]



Putin and his wife [Lyudmila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyudmila_Putina) meeting with Queen [Elizabeth II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_II), her husband [Prince Philip, Duke of Edinburgh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_Philip,_Duke_of_Edinburgh), and Prime Minister [Tony Blair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Blair) in 2005

On 4 March 2018, former double agent [Sergei Skripal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergei_Skripal) was poisoned with a [Novichok nerve agent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novichok_agent) in [Salisbury](72119.md).[^cite_note-284] Ten days later, the British government formally accused the Russian state of attempted murder, a charge which Russia denied.[^cite_note-285] After the UK expelled 23 Russian diplomats (an action which would later be responded to with a Russian expulsion of 23 British diplomats),[^cite_note-286] British [Foreign Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Foreign_and_Commonwealth_Affairs) [Boris Johnson](19065069.md) said on 16 March that it was "overwhelmingly likely" Putin had personally ordered the poisoning of Skripal. Putin's spokesman [Dmitry Peskov](9214683.md) called the allegation "shocking and unpardonable diplomatic misconduct".[^cite_note-287]

On 18 June 2020, *[The National Interest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_National_Interest)* published a nine-thousand-word essay by Putin, titled "The Real Lessons of the 75th Anniversary of World War II".[^cite_note-288] In the essay, Putin criticizes the Western historical view of the [Molotov–Ribbentrop Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molotov%E2%80%93Ribbentrop_Pact) as the start of [World War II](32927.md), stating that the [Munich Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_Agreement) was the beginning.[^cite_note-289]

On 21 February 2023, Putin suspended Russia's participation in the [New START](26709878.md) [nuclear arms reduction](22165.md) treaty with the United States.[^cite_note-290] On 25 March, President Putin announced the stationing of tactical nuclear weapons in Belarus. Russia would maintain control of the weapons. President Putin told Russian TV: "There is nothing unusual here either. Firstly, the United States has been doing this for decades. They have long deployed their tactical nuclear weapons on the territory of their allied countries."[^cite_note-291]

In August 2024, Putin pardoned American journalist [Evan Gershkovich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evan_Gershkovich), opposition figures [Vladimir Kara-Murza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Kara-Murza), [Ilya Yashin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilya_Yashin), and others in a [prisoner swap](17209653.md) with Western countries.[^cite_note-f24-292][^cite_note-tg1-293][^cite_note-rfe1-294] The [2024 Ankara prisoner exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Ankara_prisoner_exchange) was the most extensive between Russia and United States since the end of the Cold War, involving the release of 26 people.[^cite_note-295] On 22 June 2025, Putin condemned the [U.S. strikes on Iranian nuclear sites](80262844.md) as an "unprovoked act of aggression."[^cite_note-296]

### Latin America

See also: [Brazil–Russia relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazil%E2%80%93Russia_relations), [Russia–Venezuela relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia%E2%80%93Venezuela_relations), [Cuba–Russia relations](17446369.md), and [Argentina–Russia relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argentina%E2%80%93Russia_relations)



Putin and Venezuelan President [Nicolás Maduro](7161476.md) on 10 October 2019

Putin and his successor, Medvedev, enjoyed warm relations with [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez) of Venezuela. Much of this has been through the sale of military equipment; since 2005, Venezuela has purchased more than \$4 billion worth of arms from Russia.[^cite_note-298] In September 2008, Russia sent [Tupolev Tu-160](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tupolev_Tu-160) bombers to Venezuela to carry out training flights.[^cite_note-299] In November 2008, both countries held a joint naval exercise in the [Caribbean](18956035.md). Earlier in 2000, Putin had re-established stronger ties with [Fidel Castro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Castro)'s Cuba.[^cite_note-300]

"You express the best masculine qualities", Putin told [Jair Bolsonaro](46603496.md) in 2020. "You look for solutions in all matters, always putting above all the interests of your people, your country, leaving out your own personal issues". Political scientist [Oliver Stuenkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oliver_Stuenkel) noted, "Among Brazil's right-wing populists, Putin is seen as someone who is anti-[woke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woke), and that is seen as something that is definitely appealing to Bolsonaro. He is a strongman, and that is very inspiring to Bolsonaro. He would like to be someone who concentrates as much power".[^cite_note-McCoy_Harlan-301]

### Middle East and Africa

See also: [Israel–Russia relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel%E2%80%93Russia_relations), [Iran–Russia relations](3912038.md), and [Russia–South Africa relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia%E2%80%93South_Africa_relations)

On 16 October 2007, Putin visited Iran to participate in the Second Caspian Summit in [Tehran](57654.md),[^cite_note-rbc-iran-tehran-302][^cite_note-303] where he met with Iranian president [Mahmoud Ahmadinejad](7907317.md).[^cite_note-304][^cite_note-305] This was the first visit of a Soviet or Russian leader[^cite_note-306] to Iran since [Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin)'s participation in the [Tehran Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran_Conference) in 1943, and marked a significant event in [Iran–Russia relations](3912038.md).[^cite_note-307] At a press conference after the summit Putin said that "all our ([Caspian](19653787.md)) states have the right to develop their peaceful nuclear programmes without any restrictions".[^cite_note-308] Putin was quoted as describing Iran as a "partner",[^cite_note-Sturmer-248] although he expressed concerns over the [Iranian nuclear programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_nuclear_programme).[^cite_note-Sturmer-248]

In April 2008, Putin became the first Russian president to visit Libya.[^cite_note-309] Putin condemned the [2011 foreign military intervention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_military_intervention_in_Libya) in Libya, referring to the [UN resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_1973) as "defective and flawed", and added, "It allows everything. It resembles medieval calls for crusades".[^cite_note-310] Upon [the death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_Muammar_Gaddafi) of [Muammar Gaddafi](53029.md), Putin called it as "planned murder" by the US, saying: "They showed to the whole world how he (Gaddafi) was killed", and "There was blood all over. Is that what they call a democracy?"[^cite_note-311][^cite_note-312]

From 2000 to 2010, Russia sold around \$1.5 billion worth of arms to Syria, making [Damascus](8914.md) Russia's seventh-largest client.[^cite_note-313] During the Syrian civil war, Russia threatened to veto any sanctions against the Syrian government,[^cite_note-314] and continued to supply arms to its regime. Putin opposed any foreign intervention in the Syrian civil war. In June 2012, in Paris, he rejected the statement of French president [François Hollande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Hollande) who called on Bashar al-Assad to step down. Putin echoed Assad's argument that anti-regime *militants* were responsible for much of the bloodshed. He also talked about previous NATO interventions and their results, and asked, "What is happening in Libya, in Iraq? Did they become safer? Where are they heading? Nobody has an answer".[^cite_note-315]

On 11 September 2013, *[The New York Times](30680.md)* published an [op-ed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Op-ed) by Putin urging caution against [US intervention in Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American-led_intervention_in_Syria) and criticizing [American exceptionalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_exceptionalism).[^cite_note-NYT-20130911-316] Putin subsequently helped to arrange for the [destruction of Syria's chemical weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Destruction_of_Syria's_chemical_weapons).[^cite_note-317] In 2015, he took a stronger pro-Assad stance[^cite_note-318] and mobilized [military support for the regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_intervention_in_Syria). Some analysts have summarized Putin as being allied with [Shias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shias) and [Alawites](436616.md) in the Middle East.[^cite_note-319][^cite_note-320] Putin ordered Russian intervention to support his ally, Assad, to undermine the U.S.-led international order. As a result, Russia increased its influence in the [Eastern Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Mediterranean) by strengthening its control over the [Tartus Naval Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tartus_Naval_Base) and becoming an operator of the [Khmeimim Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmeimim_Air_Base).[^cite_note-321][^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323]

In 2017, Putin dispatched Russian [PMCs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_military_company) to back the [Touadéra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Touad%C3%A9ra) regime in the [Central African Republic Civil War](38024441.md), gaining a permanent military presence in return.[^cite_note-324][^cite_note-325][^cite_note-326][^cite_note-327] The first [Russia-Africa Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia%E2%80%93Africa_Summit_2019) was held in October 2019 in [Sochi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sochi), Russia, co-hosted by Putin and Egyptian president [Abdel Fattah el-Sisi](36703624.md).[^cite_note-328] The meeting was attended by 43 heads of state and government from African countries.[^cite_note-329]

In October 2019, Putin visited the United Arab Emirates, where six agreements were struck with [Abu Dhabi](18950756.md) Crown Prince [Mohammed bin Zayed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_bin_Zayed). One of them included shared investments between [Russian sovereign wealth fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_National_Wealth_Fund) and the Emirati investment fund [Mubadala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mubadala_Investment_Company). The two nations signed deals worth over \$1.3bn in the energy, health, and advanced technology sectors.[^cite_note-330] On 22 October 2021, Putin highlighted the "unique bond" between Russia and Israel during a meeting with Israeli prime minister [Naftali Bennett](39754840.md).[^cite_note-331]

## Public image

Main article: [Public image of Vladimir Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_image_of_Vladimir_Putin)

### Polls and rankings

In a June 2007 public opinion survey, Putin's approval rating was 81%, the second-highest of any leader in the world that year.[^cite_note-333] In January 2013, at the time of the [2011–2013 Russian protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011%E2%80%932013_Russian_protests), Putin's rating fell to 62%, the lowest since 2000.[^cite_note-334] In a context of increased diplomatic isolation and international sanctions on Russian officials prompted by the Russo-Ukrainian war, Putin's approval rating reached 87% in August 2014.[^cite_note-335] In June 2015, Putin's approval rating climbed to 89%, an all-time high.[^cite_note-guardian-336][^cite_note-337][^cite_note-338] Observers saw Putin's high approval ratings in 2010s as a consequence of improvements in living standards, and Russia's reassertion on the world scene during his presidency.[^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340] The director of the [Levada Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levada_Center) stated in 2015 that drawing conclusions from Russian poll results or comparing them to polls in democratic states was irrelevant, as there is no real political competition in Russia, where, unlike in democratic states, Russian voters are not offered any credible alternatives and public opinion is primarily formed by state-controlled media, which promotes those in power and discredits alternative candidates.[^cite_note-341]

Putin's performance in reining in corruption is unpopular among Russians. *[Newsweek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newsweek)* reported in 2017 that a poll "indicated that 67% held Putin personally responsible for high-level corruption".[^cite_note-Newsweek-342] Corruption [is a significant problem in Russia](27352290.md).[^cite_note-343][^cite_note-344] In October 2018, two-thirds of Russians surveyed agreed that "Putin bears full responsibility for the problems of the country", which has been attributed[^cite_note-345] to a decline in a popular belief in "[good tsar and bad boyars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_tsar,_bad_boyars)", a traditional attitude towards justifying failures at the top of the ruling hierarchy in Russia.[^cite_note-346] In January 2019, the percentage of Russians trusting Putin hit a then-historic low—33%.[^cite_note-347] In May 2020, amid the COVID crisis, Putin's approval rating was 68%, when respondents were presented a list of names (closed question),[^cite_note-348] and 27% when respondents were expected to name politicians they trust (open question).[^cite_note-349] This has been attributed to continued post-Crimea economic stagnation but also an apathetic response to the pandemic crisis in Russia.[^cite_note-350]

Following the Russian invasion of Ukraine in 2022, state-controlled TV, where most Russians get their news, presented the invasion as a "special military operation" and liberation mission, in line with the government's narrative.[^cite_note-351][^cite_note-masha-gessen-invasion-352][^cite_note-353] The Russian censorship apparatus *[Roskomnadzor](15773661.md)* ordered the country's media to employ information only from state sources or face fines and blocks.[^cite_note-354] The Russian media was banned from using the words "war", "invasion" or "aggression" to describe the invasion,[^cite_note-masha-gessen-invasion-352] with media outlets being blocked as a result.[^cite_note-nyt-approval-march-2022-355] In March 2022, 97% of Ukrainians said they had an unfavorable view of Putin, and 98% of Ukrainians—including 82% of [ethnic Russians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russians_in_Ukraine) living in Ukraine—said they did not believe any part of Ukraine was rightfully part of Russia.[^cite_note-356]

In late February 2022, a survey conducted by the independent research group Russian Field found that 59% of respondents supported the "special military operation" in Ukraine.[^cite_note-opendemocracy-357] In late February and mid-March 2022 two polls surveyed Russians' sentiments about the "special military operation" in Ukraine. The results were obtained by [Radio Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Liberty).[^cite_note-lib-358] 71% of Russians polled said that they supported the "special military operation" in Ukraine.[^cite_note-Belsat_TV-2022-359][^cite_note-lib-358] A poll published on 30 March in Russia saw Putin's approval rating jump, from 71% in February, to 83%.[^cite_note-360][^cite_note-361] However, experts warned that the figures may not accurately reflect the public mood, as the public tends to rally around leaders during war and some may be hiding their true opinions,[^cite_note-362] especially with the [Russian 2022 war censorship laws](70646883.md) prohibiting dissemination of "fake information" about the military.[^cite_note-363] Many respondents do not want to answer pollsters' questions for fear of negative consequences.[^cite_note-opendemocracy-357] When researchers commissioned a survey on Russians' attitudes to the war, 29,400 out of 31,000 refused to answer.[^cite_note-364] The Levada Center's director stated that early feelings of "shock and confusion" were being replaced with the belief that Russia was being besieged and that Russians must rally around their leader.[^cite_note-nyt-approval-march-2022-355] The Kremlin's analysis concluded that public support for the war was broad but not deep, and that most Russians would accept anything Putin labeled a victory. In September 2023, the head of the [VTsIOM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Public_Opinion_Research_Center) state pollster Valery Fyodorov said in an interview that only 10–15% of Russians actively supported the war, and that "most Russians are not demanding the conquest of Kyiv or Odesa".[^cite_note-NYTimes-2023-12-23-365]
