### Cult of personality

Main article: [Public image of Vladimir Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_image_of_Vladimir_Putin)

See also: [List of cults of personality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cults_of_personality)



Putin driving a [Formula One car](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formula_One_car), 2010 (video)

Putin has cultivated a cult of personality for himself[^cite_note-368][^cite_note-369][^cite_note-370] with an outdoorsy, athletic, [tough guy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macho) [public image](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_image_of_Vladimir_Putin), demonstrating his physical prowess and taking part in unusual or dangerous acts, such as extreme sports and interaction with wild animals,[^cite_note-abcnews.go.com-371] part of a public relations approach that, according to *[Wired](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wired_\(magazine\))*, "deliberately cultivates the macho, take-charge [superhero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superhero) image".[^cite_note-Superputin-372] In 2007, the tabloid *[Komsomolskaya Pravda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Komsomolskaya_Pravda)* published a huge photograph of a shirtless Putin vacationing in the Siberian mountains under the headline "Be Like Putin".[^cite_note-putin-shirtless-373] Tatiana Mikhailova opines that virility is an aspect of the image of the *Father of the Nation*, which Putin wants to create. She refers to, as an example of the success of this image building attempt, a 2006 incident in which Putin lifted the shirt of a boy to kiss his stomach without permission – which did not cause much reaction in Russia even though, according to Mikhailova, it was unprecedented and transgressive by Russian standards and would have caused outrage in any other country (media reports note that there was widespread reaction in Russia and abroad, though).[^cite_note-374][^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376]

Numerous [Kremlinologists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kremlinologists) have accused Putin of seeking to create a cult of personality around himself, an accusation that the Kremlin has denied.[^cite_note-377] Some of Putin's activities have been criticised for being staged;[^cite_note-amphorae-378][^cite_note-Fishy-379] outside of Russia, his macho image has been the subject of parody.[^cite_note-380][^cite_note-381][^cite_note-382] Putin's height has been estimated by Kremlin insiders to be between 155 and 165 centimetres (5 feet 1 inch and 5 feet 5 inches) tall but is usually given at 170 centimetres (5 feet 7 inches).[^cite_note-height-383][^cite_note-384]

There are many songs about Putin,[^cite_note-385] and Putin's name and image are widely used in advertisements and product branding.[^cite_note-Superputin-372] Among the Putin-branded products are [Putinka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Putinka) vodka, the [PuTin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PuTin_\(brand\)) brand of canned food, the *Gorbusha Putina* [caviar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caviar), and a collection of T-shirts with his image.[^cite_note-386]

### Public recognition in the West

In 2007, he was the [*Time* Person of the Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_Person_of_the_Year).[^cite_note-387][^cite_note-388] In 2015, he was No. 1 on the [*Time's* Most Influential People List](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_100).[^cite_note-389][^cite_note-390] *[Forbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes)* ranked him the [World's Most Powerful Individual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes_list_of_The_World's_Most_Powerful_People) every year from 2013 to 2016.[^cite_note-391] He was ranked the second-most-powerful individual by *Forbes* in 2018 the last year the list has been published.[^cite_note-392]

In Germany, the word "[Putinversteher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Putinversteher)" (female form "Putinversteherin") is a [neologism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neologism) and a political buzzword (*Putin* + *[verstehen](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/verstehen)*), which literally translates "Putin understander", i.e., "one who understands Putin".[^cite_note-umland2016-393] It is a pejorative reference to politicians and pundits who express [empathy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empathy) to Putin and may also be translated as "Putin-empathizer".[^cite_note-gregory-394]

### Putinisms

Putin has produced many aphorisms and catch-phrases known as [putinisms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Putinisms).[^cite_note-putinisms-395] Many of them were first made during his annual Q\&A conferences, where Putin answered questions from journalists and other people in the studio, as well as from Russians throughout the country, who either phoned in or spoke from studios and outdoor sites across Russia. Putin is known for his often tough and sharp language, often alluding to [Russian jokes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_jokes) and folk sayings.[^cite_note-putinisms-395] Putin sometimes uses Russian criminal jargon (known as "[fenya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fenya)" in Russian), albeit not always correctly.[^cite_note-396]

## Assessments



[Z symbol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Z_\(military_symbol\)) on a billboard reads Russian: За Путина (lit. 'For Putin'), 24 September 2022

Assessments of Putin's character as a leader have evolved during his long presidency. His shifting of Russia towards autocracy and weakening of the system of representative government advocated by [Boris Yeltsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Yeltsin) has met with criticism.[^cite_note-397] Russian dissidents and western leaders now frequently characterise him as a dictator. Others have offered favourable assessments of his impact on Russia.

[Otto von Habsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_von_Habsburg), the last [crown prince](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_heirs_to_the_Austrian_throne) of [Austria-Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austria-Hungary) and former [Member of the European Parliament](258909.md), was an early critic of Putin. In a newspaper interview[^cite_note-398] in 2002 and in two speeches[^cite_note-399] in 2003 and 2005, he warned of Putin as an "international threat", that he was "cruel and oppressive", and a "stone cold technocrat".[^cite_note-400]

Putin was described in 2015 as a "dictator" by political opponent [Garry Kasparov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garry_Kasparov),[^cite_note-401] and as the "Tsar of corruption" in 2016 by opposition activist and blogger [Alexei Navalny](29174356.md).[^cite_note-402] He was described as a "bully" and "arrogant" by former U.S. secretary of state [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton),[^cite_note-403][^cite_note-404][^cite_note-405] and as "self-centered" by the [Dalai Lama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dalai_Lama).[^cite_note-406] In 2015, opposition politician Boris Nemtsov said that Putin was turning Russia into a "raw materials colony" of China.[^cite_note-407]

Former U.S. secretary of state [Henry Kissinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Kissinger) wrote in 2014 that the West has demonized Putin.[^cite_note-408] [Egon Krenz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egon_Krenz), former leader of East Germany, said the Cold War never ended, adding: "After weak presidents like [Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev) and Yeltsin, it is a great fortune for Russia that it has Putin".[^cite_note-409]

Many Russians credit Putin for reviving Russia's fortunes.[^cite_note-International_Business_Times-2014-410] Former Soviet Union leader Mikhail Gorbachev, while acknowledging the flawed democratic procedures and restrictions on media freedom during the Putin presidency, said that Putin had pulled Russia out of chaos at the end of the Yeltsin years, and that Russians "must remember that Putin saved Russia from the beginning of a collapse".[^cite_note-International_Business_Times-2014-410][^cite_note-411] [Chechen Republic](6095.md) head and Putin supporter, [Ramzan Kadyrov](647880.md), stated prior to 2011 that Putin saved both the Chechen people and Russia.[^cite_note-412]

Russia has suffered [democratic backsliding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding) during Putin's tenure.[^cite_note-413] [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House) has listed Russia as being "not free" since 2005.[^cite_note-414] Experts do not generally consider Russia to be a democracy,[^cite_note-Gill-20162-36][^cite_note-Diamond-2015-415][^cite_note-Levitsky-2010-416] citing purges and jailing of political opponents,[^cite_note-Reuter-20172-37][^cite_note-417] curtailed press freedom,[^cite_note-418][^cite_note-419][^cite_note-420] and the lack of free and fair elections.[^cite_note-421][^cite_note-422][^cite_note-423] In 2004, Freedom House warned that Russia's "retreat from freedom marks a low point not registered since 1989, when the country was part of the Soviet Union".[^cite_note-424]

The [Economist Intelligence Unit](15303116.md) has rated Russia as "authoritarian" since 2011,[^cite_note-425][^cite_note-426] whereas it had previously been considered a "[hybrid regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hybrid_regime)" (with "some form of democratic government" in place).[^cite_note-Democracy-427] According to political scientist Larry Diamond, writing in 2015, "no serious scholar would consider Russia today a democracy".[^cite_note-Diamond-2015-415]

Following the jailing of the anti-corruption blogger and activist [Alexei Navalny](29174356.md) in 2018, *[Forbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes)* wrote: "Putin's actions are those of a dictator... As a leader with failing public support, he can only remain in power by using force and repression that gets worse by the day".[^cite_note-428] In November 2021, *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* also noted that Putin had "shifted from autocracy to dictatorship".[^cite_note-429]

In a 2015 opinion article for *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)*, former U.S. ambassador to Germany [John Kornblum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kornblum) disputed Putin's claim that Russia had been betrayed by the West after the Cold War. Kornblum argued that Western governments had not intended to humiliate Russia and had observed the agreements reached with Moscow in the early 1990s.[^cite_note-jkwsj-430]

In her 2017 book *Red Hangover: Legacies of Twentieth-Century Communism*, [Kristen Ghodsee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kristen_Ghodsee) argued that the triumphalist attitudes of Western powers at the end of the [Cold War](325329.md), and the fixation with linking all leftist and [socialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist) political ideals with the horrors of [Stalinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stalinism), allowed neoliberalism to fill the void, undermined democratic institutions and reforms, left a trail of economic misery, unemployment, hopelessness and rising inequality throughout the former [Eastern Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc). This includes Russia, helping fuel the rise of Putin's extremist right-wing nationalism.[^cite_note-431]

### After the 2022 invasion of Ukraine



Protest sign in front of the Russian embassy in Finland. Putin has been labeled a [war criminal](70167888.md) by international law experts.

[^cite_note-432]

Following mounting civilian casualties during the Russian invasion of Ukraine,[^cite_note-433] U.S. president [Joe Biden](145422.md) called Putin a war criminal and "murderous dictator".[^cite_note-Putin_according_to_Biden-434][^cite_note-435] In the [2022 State of the Union Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_State_of_the_Union_Address), Biden said that Putin had "badly miscalculated".[^cite_note-436] The [Ukrainian envoy to the United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_Representative_of_Ukraine_to_the_United_Nations), [Sergiy Kyslytsya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergiy_Kyslytsya) likened Putin to [Adolf Hitler](2731583.md).[^cite_note-437] Latvian prime minister [Krisjanis Karins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krisjanis_Karins) also likened the Russian leader to Hitler, saying he was "a deluded autocrat creating misery for millions" and that "Putin is fighting against democracy (...) If he can attack Ukraine, theoretically it could be any other European country".[^cite_note-438][^cite_note-439]

Lithuania's foreign minister, [Gabrielius Landsbergis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabrielius_Landsbergis), stated, "The battle for Ukraine is a battle for Europe. If Putin is not stopped there, he will go further."[^cite_note-440] French President [Emmanuel Macron](43671127.md) said Putin was "deluding himself".[^cite_note-441] French foreign minister [Jean-Yves Le Drian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Yves_Le_Drian) denounced him as "a cynic and a dictator".[^cite_note-442] Canadian Prime Minister [Justin Trudeau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justin_Trudeau) called Putin "a lying, murderous dictator".[^cite_note-443] UK Prime Minister [Boris Johnson](19065069.md) also labelled Putin a "dictator" who had authorised "a tidal wave of violence against a fellow Slavic people".[^cite_note-444] Several authors and academics, such as [Michael Hirsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Hirsh_\(journalist\)), described Putin as a "messianic" [Russian nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_nationalism) and [Eurasianist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasianism).[^cite_note-445][^cite_note-446][^cite_note-447]

In a March 2025 essay in *[The Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Atlantic)*, Franklin Foer argued that Putin's rule had had consequences beyond Russia, including for democratic development in neighboring states and for relations between Russia and the West.[^cite_note-448]

## Electoral history

Main article: [Electoral history of Vladimir Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_history_of_Vladimir_Putin)

Vladimir Putin has been nominated and elected as President of Russia all five times since 2000, typically under an [independent](2172378.md) banner. In the most recent 2024 Russian presidential election, Putin achieved 88% of the popular vote.[^cite_note-449] There were reports of [irregularities](382165.md) at this election,[^cite_note-450] including [ballot stuffing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballot_stuffing) and coercion.[^cite_note-MoscowTimesGolos2024-35][^cite_note-451] Russian authorities claimed that in occupied areas of Ukraine's [Zaporizhzhia](494936.md) and [Kherson](1273370.md) regions,[^cite_note-452] Putin won 88.12% and 92.83% of votes.[^cite_note-453] In [Chechnya](6095.md), Putin won 98.99% of the vote.[^cite_note-Le_Monde-454]

## Personal life

### Family

Main article: [Family of Vladimir Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_of_Vladimir_Putin)



Putin and [Lyudmila Putina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyudmila_Putina) during their wedding on 28 July 1983

On 28 July 1983, Putin married [Lyudmila Shkrebneva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyudmila_Putina), and they lived together in East Germany from 1985 to 1990. They have two daughters, [Maria Putina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maria_Vorontsova), born on 28 April 1985 in [Leningrad](24320051.md) (now Saint Petersburg), and [Yekaterina Putina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katerina_Tikhonova), born on 31 August 1986 in Dresden, East Germany (now Germany).[^cite_note-455]

An investigation by *[Proekt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proekt)* published in November 2020 alleged that Putin has another daughter, Elizaveta, also known as Luiza Rozova,[^cite_note-456] (born in March 2003),[^cite_note-457] with [Svetlana Krivonogikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Svetlana_Krivonogikh).[^cite_note-Proekt201125-11][^cite_note-458] Elizaveta studied in [Paris](22989.md) under the name Elizaveta Olegovna Rudnova.[^cite_note-459] In April 2008, the *[Moskovsky Korrespondent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moskovsky_Korrespondent)* reported that Putin had divorced Lyudmila and was engaged to marry Olympic gold medalist [Alina Kabaeva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alina_Kabaeva), a former rhythmic gymnast and Russian politician.[^cite_note-RFERL080418-8] The story was denied,[^cite_note-RFERL080418-8] and the newspaper was shut down shortly thereafter.[^cite_note-NYT120505-9] Putin and Lyudmila continued to make public appearances together as spouses,[^cite_note-460][^cite_note-461] while the status of his relationship with Kabaeva became a topic of speculation.[^cite_note-Times150314-462]

On 6 June 2013, Putin and Lyudmila announced that their marriage was over; on 1 April 2014, the Kremlin confirmed that the divorce had been finalised.[^cite_note-463][^cite_note-464][^cite_note-465] Kabaeva reportedly gave birth to a daughter by Putin in 2015;[^cite_note-NEWSru150519-466][^cite_note-NW160202-467] this report was denied.[^cite_note-NEWSru150519-466] Kabaeva reportedly gave birth to twin sons by Putin in 2019.[^cite_note-Times190526-12][^cite_note-468] However, in 2022, Swiss media, citing the couple's Swiss gynecologist, wrote that on both occasions Kabaeva gave birth to a boy.[^cite_note-SonntagsZeitung-13]

Putin has two grandsons, born in 2012 and 2017,[^cite_note-469][^cite_note-470] through Maria.[^cite_note-471] He reportedly also has a granddaughter, born in 2017, through Katerina.[^cite_note-472][^cite_note-473] His cousin, [Igor Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igor_Putin), was a director at Moscow-based [Master Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Master_Bank) and was accused in a number of money-laundering scandals.[^cite_note-474][^cite_note-475]

### Wealth

See also: [Panama Papers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panama_Papers) and [Pandora Papers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pandora_Papers)

Official figures released during the [legislative election of 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Russian_legislative_election) put Putin's wealth at approximately 3.7 million [rubles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_ruble) (US\$280,000) in bank accounts, a private 77.4-square-meter (833 sq ft) apartment in Saint Petersburg, and miscellaneous other assets.[^cite_note-476][^cite_note-477] Putin's reported 2006 income totaled 2 million rubles (approximately \$152,000). In 2012, Putin reported an income of 3.6 million rubles (\$270,000).[^cite_note-478][^cite_note-479] Putin has been photographed wearing several expensive wristwatches, collectively valued at \$700,000, nearly six times his annual salary.[^cite_note-ABCWatches2012-480][^cite_note-481] Putin has been known on occasion to give watches valued at thousands of dollars as gifts, for example a watch identified as a [Blancpain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blancpain) to a Siberian boy he met while on vacation in 2009, and another similar watch to a factory worker the same year.[^cite_note-ABCWatches2012-480]



Putin's close associate [Arkady Rotenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arkady_Rotenberg) mentioned in the Panama Papers, pictured in 2018

According to Russian opposition politicians and journalists,[^cite_note-482][^cite_note-Guardian_40bn-483] Putin secretly possesses a multi-billion-dollar fortune via successive ownership of stakes in several Russian companies.[^cite_note-Is-484][^cite_note-485] According to one editorial in *[The Washington Post](102226.md)*, "Putin might not technically own these 43 aircraft, but, as the sole political power in Russia, he can act like they're his".[^cite_note-486] An [RIA Novosti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RIA_Novosti) journalist argued that "\[Western] intelligence agencies ... could not find anything". These contradictory claims were analyzed by [Polygraph.info](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polygraph.info),[^cite_note-487] which looked at several reports by Western ([Anders Åslund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anders_%C3%85slund) estimate of \$100–160 billion) and Russian ([Stanislav Belkovsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanislav_Belkovsky) estimated of \$40 billion) analysts, [CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA) (estimate of \$40 billion in 2007) as well as counterarguments of Russian media. Polygraph concluded:

> There is uncertainty on the precise sum of Putin's wealth, and the assessment by the Director of U.S. National Intelligence apparently is not yet complete. However, with the pile of evidence and documents in the Panama Papers and in the hands of independent investigators such as those cited by Dawisha, Polygraph.info finds that Danilov's claim that Western intelligence agencies have not been able to find evidence of Putin's wealth to be misleading

— Polygraph.info, "Are 'Putin's Billions' a Myth?"

In April 2016, 11 million documents belonging to Panamanian law firm [Mossack Fonseca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mossack_Fonseca) were [leaked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_people_named_in_the_Panama_Papers) to the German newspaper *[Süddeutsche Zeitung](30873120.md)* and the [Washington](108956.md)-based [International Consortium of Investigative Journalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_Public_Integrity). The name of Putin does not appear in any of the records, and Putin denied his involvement with the company.[^cite_note-488] However, various media have reported on three of Putin's associates on the list.[^cite_note-hardingrevealed-489] According to the [Panama Papers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panama_Papers) leak, close trusted associates of Putin own offshore companies worth US\$2 billion in total.[^cite_note-490] *Süddeutsche Zeitung* regards the possibility of Putin's family profiting from this money as plausible.[^cite_note-491][^cite_note-hardingrevealed-489]

According to the paper, the US\$2 billion had been "secretly shuffled through banks and shadow companies linked to Putin's associates", such as construction billionaires [Arkady](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arkady_Rotenberg) and [Boris Rotenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Romanovich_Rotenberg), and [Bank Rossiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rossiya_Bank), previously identified by the U.S. State Department as being treated by Putin as his personal bank account, had been central in facilitating this. It concludes that "Putin has shown he is willing to take aggressive steps to maintain secrecy and protect \[such] communal assets".[^cite_note-492][^cite_note-493]

A significant proportion of the money trail leads to Putin's best friend [Sergei Roldugin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergei_Roldugin). Although a musician, and in his own words, not a businessman, it appears he has accumulated assets valued at \$100m, and possibly more. It has been suggested he was picked for the role because of his low profile.[^cite_note-hardingrevealed-489] There have been speculations that Putin, in fact, owns the funds,[^cite_note-494] and Roldugin just acted as a proxy.[^cite_note-495] [Garry Kasparov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garry_Kasparov) said that "\[Putin] controls enough money, probably more than any other individual in the history of human race".[^cite_note-496]

### Residences

#### Official government residences



Putin receives [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) at his residence in [Novo-Ogaryovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novo-Ogaryovo) in 2009

As president and prime minister, Putin has lived in numerous official residences throughout the country.[^cite_note-497] These residences include: the [Moscow Kremlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Kremlin), [Novo-Ogaryovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novo-Ogaryovo) in [Moscow Oblast](372315.md), Gorki-9 near Moscow, [Bocharov Ruchey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bocharov_Ruchey) in [Sochi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sochi), [Dolgiye Borody](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolgiye_Borody_\(residence\)) in [Novgorod Oblast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novgorod_Oblast), and Riviera in Sochi.[^cite_note-residences-498] In August 2012, critics of Putin listed the ownership of 20 villas and palaces, nine of which were built during Putin's 12 years in power.[^cite_note-Slave-499]

#### Personal residences

Soon after Putin returned from his KGB service in Dresden, East Germany, he built a [dacha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dacha) in Solovyovka on the eastern shore of Lake Komsomolskoye on the [Karelian Isthmus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karelian_Isthmus) in [Priozersky District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Priozersky_District) of [Leningrad Oblast](480845.md), near St. Petersburg. After the dacha burned down in 1996, Putin built a new one identical to the original and was joined by a group of seven friends who built dachas nearby. In 1996, the group formally registered their fraternity as a [co-operative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housing_cooperative) society, calling it [Ozero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ozero) ("Lake") and turning it into a [gated community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gated_community).[^cite_note-500]

A massive Italianate-style mansion costing an alleged US\$1 billion[^cite_note-'Putin_palace'_sold-501] and dubbed "[Putin's Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Putin's_Palace)" is under construction near the Black Sea village of Praskoveevka. In 2012, [Sergei Kolesnikov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergei_Kolesnikov_\(whistleblower\)), a former business associate of Putin's, told the BBC's *[Newsnight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newsnight)* programme that he had been ordered by Deputy Prime Minister [Igor Sechin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igor_Sechin) to oversee the building of the palace.[^cite_note-Putin's_palace-502] He also said that the mansion, built on government land and sporting three helipads, plus a private road paid for from state funds and guarded by officials wearing uniforms of the official Kremlin guard service, have been built for Putin's private use.[^cite_note-IBP-2014-503]

On 19 January 2021, two days after [Alexei Navalny](29174356.md) was detained by Russian authorities upon his return to Russia, a video investigation by him and the [Anti-Corruption Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Corruption_Foundation) (FBK) was published, accusing Putin of using fraudulently obtained funds to build the estate for himself in what he called "the world's biggest bribe". In the investigation, Navalny said that the estate is 39 times the size of Monaco and costs over 100 billion rubles (\$1.35 billion) to construct. It also showed aerial footage of the estate via a drone and a detailed floorplan of the palace that Navalny said was given by a contractor, which he compared to photographs from inside the palace that were leaked onto the Internet in 2011. He also detailed an elaborate corruption scheme allegedly involving Putin's inner circle that allowed Putin to hide billions of dollars to build the estate.[^cite_note-tmt-putin's-palace-504][^cite_note-505][^cite_note-506] Since the [prelude to the Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prelude_to_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine), Putin prefers to travel in an armored train to flying.[^cite_note-507]

### Pets

Main article: [Pets of Vladimir Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pets_of_Vladimir_Putin)



Putin's pet, named Verni, was a birthday gift from [Gurbanguly Berdimuhamedow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurbanguly_Berdimuhamedow), president of Turkmenistan, during a meeting in Sochi in October 2017.

Putin has received five dogs from various national leaders: [Konni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konni_\(dog\)), Buffy, Yume, Verni and Pasha. Konni died in 2014. When Putin first became president, the family had two poodles, Tosya and Rodeo. They reportedly stayed with his ex-wife [Lyudmila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyudmila_Putina) after their divorce.[^cite_note-508]

### Religion



Putin and wife Lyudmila in New York at a service for victims of the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md), 16 November 2001

Putin is [Russian Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church). His mother was a devout Christian who attended the Russian Orthodox Church; his father was an [atheist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheist).[^cite_note-Colton-509] Although his mother kept no [icons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Icon) at home, she attended church regularly, despite government persecution of her faith at that time. His mother secretly baptized him as a baby, and she regularly took him to services.[^cite_note-sakwa_p3-510]

According to Putin, his religious awakening began after a serious car crash involving his wife in 1993, and a life-threatening fire that burned down their [dacha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dacha) in August 1996.[^cite_note-Colton-509] Shortly before an official visit to Israel, Putin's mother gave him his baptismal cross, telling him to get it blessed. Putin has stated, "I did as she said and then put the cross around my neck. I have never taken it off since".[^cite_note-sakwa_p3-510]

When asked in 2007 whether he believes in God, he replied, "There are things I believe, which should not in my position, at least, be shared with the public at large for everybody's consumption because that would look like [self-advertising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-advertising) or a political [striptease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Striptease)".[^cite_note-511] Putin's rumoured [confessor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confessor) is Russian Orthodox bishop [Tikhon Shevkunov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tikhon_\(Shevkunov\)).[^cite_note-FT-512] The sincerity of his Christianity has been rejected by his former advisor [Sergei Pugachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergei_Pugachev).[^cite_note-veconomist-513]

### Sports



Putin practicing judo in Tokyo, Japan, in September 2000

Putin watches [football](10568.md) and supports [FC Zenit Saint Petersburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FC_Zenit_Saint_Petersburg).[^cite_note-Putin_to_talk_pipeline,_attend_football_game-514] He also displays an interest in [ice hockey](14790.md) and [bandy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandy),[^cite_note-515] and played in a star-studded hockey game on his 63rd birthday.[^cite_note-516]

Putin has been practicing [judo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judo) since he was 11,[^cite_note-kremlinbiosports-517] before switching to [sambo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sambo_\(martial_art\)) at the age of fourteen.[^cite_note-518] He won competitions in both sports in [Leningrad](24320051.md) (now Saint Petersburg). He was awarded eighth [dan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_\(rank\)) of the [black belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_belt_\(martial_arts\)) in 2012, becoming the first Russian to achieve the status.[^cite_note-519] He was rewarded an eighth-degree karate black belt in 2014.[^cite_note-520]

He co-authored a book titled *Learn Judo with Vladimir Putin* in Russian (2000),[^cite_note-521] and *Judo: History, Theory, Practice* in English (2004).[^cite_note-putin-judo-522] [Benjamin Wittes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Wittes), a black belt in taekwondo and aikido and editor of *[Lawfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawfare_\(website\))*, has disputed Putin's martial arts skills, stating that there is no video evidence of Putin displaying any real noteworthy judo skills.[^cite_note-523][^cite_note-524]

In March 2022, Putin was removed from all positions in the [International Judo Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Judo_Federation) (IJF) due to the Russo-Ukrainian war.[^cite_note-525]

### Health

See also: [Claims of Vladimir Putin's incapacity and death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claims_of_Vladimir_Putin's_incapacity_and_death)

In July 2022, the director of the U.S. [Central Intelligence Agency](5183633.md), [William Burns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_J._Burns_\(diplomat\)), stated they had no evidence to suggest Putin was unstable or in bad health. The statement was made because of increasing unconfirmed media speculation about Putin's health. Burns had previously been [U.S. ambassador to Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Ambassador_to_Russia), and had personally observed Putin for over two decades, including a personal meeting in November 2021. A Kremlin spokesperson also dismissed rumours of Putin's bad health.[^cite_note-bbc-20220721-526]

The Russian political magazine *Sobesednik* ([Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language): *Собеседник*) alleged in 2018 that Putin had a [sensory room](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sensory_room) installed in [his private residence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolgiye_Borody_\(residence\)) in the [Novgorod Oblast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novgorod_Oblast).[^cite_note-527] The [White House](33057.md), as well as Western generals, politicians, and [political analysts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_analysts), have questioned Putin's [mental health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mental_disorder) after two years of isolation during the COVID-19 pandemic.[^cite_note-528][^cite_note-529][^cite_note-530]

In April 2022, London tabloid newspaper *[The Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sun_\(United_Kingdom\))* asserted that based on video footage Putin may have [Parkinson's disease](22228064.md).[^cite_note-DW-531][^cite_note-532][^cite_note-Roth-533] This speculation, which has not been supported by medical professionals, has spread in part due to Russia's invasion of Ukraine, which many saw as an irrational act.[^cite_note-Roth-533] The [Kremlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kremlin)[^cite_note-DW-531] rejected the possibility of Parkinson's along with outside medical professionals, who stress that it is impossible to diagnose the condition based on video clips alone.[^cite_note-Roth-533]

## Awards and honours

Main article: [List of awards and honours received by Vladimir Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_awards_and_honours_received_by_Vladimir_Putin)

At least fifteen countries have awarded Vladimir Putin civilian honors since 2001. Putin has been awarded [honorary doctorates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honorary_doctorate) and other awards from organizations across the world, but some of these were revoked after the 2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine.[^cite_note-534]

In Russia, national governance represents outright authoritarianism, dominated by widespread oppression and large-scale corruption among the top elites. The 2022 invasion of Ukraine has set the Russian regime on a further downward spiral, making it one of the most notorious personalist dictatorships in the world.
